# Exam 2: In-House Step 1 Bank 2 (275 Questions - Randomized)

All questions have been audited and cleaned for option length parity, eliminated giveaway parentheticals/punctuation, and randomized across all 5 modules.

### Category Summary
- **Visual Reflexes**: 55 questions
- **UMN / LMN**: 55 questions
- **Visual System**: 55 questions
- **Auditory & Vestibular Systems**: 55 questions
- **Sleep & Sleep Disorders**: 55 questions

**Total Questions**: 275

---

## Question 1 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q8`

A 68-year-old male suffers an acute ischemic stroke involving the right Frontal Eye Field (FEF) in the precentral gyrus. On physical examination, toward which side will the patient's eyes preferentially deviate at rest?

- **(A)** Toward the side of the lesion and away from the hemiplegia ✅
- **(B)** Away from the side of the lesion and toward the hemiplegia
- **(C)** Pure upward vertical deviation toward the superior orbital rim
- **(D)** Fixed bilateral inward convergence toward the nasal midline
- **(E)** Rapid downbeat vertical nystagmus with conjugate oscillopsia

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The frontal eye field (FEF), located in the posterior portion of the middle frontal gyrus / precentral cortex (Brodmann area 8), generates voluntary horizontal conjugate saccades to the contralateral side by projecting across the midline to the contralateral paramedian pontine reticular formation (PPRF). An acute destructive stroke involving the right FEF abolishes the drive to look leftward. Unopposed input from the intact left FEF drives horizontal gaze toward the right side. Consequently, at rest, the patient's eyes preferentially deviate TOWARD the side of the hemispheric cortical lesion (to the right) and AWAY from the side of contralateral hemiplegia.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Destructive cortical FEF lesions cause conjugate eye deviation TOWARD the lesion (away from the hemiplegia); pontine PPRF lesions cause deviation AWAY from the lesion.

---

## Question 2 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q6`

An investigator conducts an experiment evaluating descending motor control in non-human primates. A localized stereotactic lesion is made in the red nucleus of the midbrain. The physiological changes in muscle tone and posture are subsequently measured. Which descending pathway is directly disrupted by this lesion, and what is its primary physiological role in modulating motor function?

- **(A)** Reticulospinal tract; facilitation of axial extensor muscle tone
- **(B)** Rubrospinal tract; suppression of stretch reflexes and modulation of flexor tone ✅
- **(C)** Lateral corticospinal tract; execution of fine motor movements of the distal digits
- **(D)** Anterior corticospinal tract; bilateral innervation of axial trunk musculature
- **(E)** Vestibulospinal tract; maintenance of posture against gravity

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Upper motor neurons that modulate movement originate in the cortex and brainstem nuclei (e.g., red nucleus giving rise to the rubrospinal tract, reticular formation giving rise to reticulospinal tracts). These brainstem descending pathways act alongside corticospinal projections to suppress the monosynaptic stretch reflex circuit and reduce resting muscle tone. Interruption removes descending inhibition, contributing to hyperreflexia and spasticity. Option (A) misattributes reticulospinal origin to the red nucleus; Option (C) describes lateral corticospinal tract; Option (D) describes anterior corticospinal tract; Option (E) describes vestibulospinal tract from vestibular nuclei.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Rubrospinal tract; suppression of stretch reflexes and modulation of flexor tone

---

## Question 3 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q48`

A 40-year-old female presents with a Horner syndrome on the right side. The clinician considers potential lesion sites along the three-neuron sympathetic chain. Which of the following lesions can cause an ipsilateral Horner syndrome?

- **(A)** Lateral medullary stroke causing ipsilateral Horner and contralateral pain loss ✅
- **(B)** Superior pulmonary sulcus tumor invading the brachial plexus and thoracic outlet
- **(C)** Internal carotid artery dissection causing ipsilateral periorbital neck pain
- **(D)** Cavernous sinus thrombosis causing multilesional extraocular ophthalmoplegia
- **(E)** Stylomastoid foramen peripheral facial nerve trauma causing facial paralysis

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Lateral medullary infarction (Wallenberg syndrome) is classically caused by occlusion of the posterior inferior cerebellar artery (PICA). The infarct damages the uncrossed descending hypothalamospinal tract in the lateral medullary tegmentum, producing an ipsilateral Horner syndrome (ptosis, miosis, anhidrosis). Simultaneously, it interrupts the adjacent anterolateral spinothalamic tract, resulting in loss of pain and temperature sensation over the CONTRALATERAL body. This crossed sensory finding combined with ipsilateral Horner syndrome localizes the lesion unequivocally to the brainstem.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Lateral medullary stroke (Wallenberg) causes ipsilateral Horner syndrome alongside contralateral loss of pain and temperature sensation over the trunk and extremities.

---

## Question 4 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q21`

A 38-year-old female with a history of hypertension presents with an acute stroke involving the caudal pons on the left side, destroying the left abducens nucleus. How does a lesion of the left abducens nucleus differ clinically from an isolated lesion of the left abducens nerve root?

- **(A)** Abducens nucleus lesion causes horizontal gaze palsy; nerve root lesion causes isolated abduction failure ✅
- **(B)** Abducens nucleus lesion causes upward vertical gaze palsy; nerve root lesion causes lateral gaze palsy
- **(C)** Abducens nucleus lesion spares extraocular movements; nerve root lesion causes complete ophthalmoplegia
- **(D)** Abducens nucleus lesion causes contralateral eyelid ptosis; nerve root lesion causes miosis and anhidrosis
- **(E)** Abducens nucleus lesion causes bilateral pupillary mydriasis; nerve root lesion spares all pupillary responses

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The abducens nucleus in the caudal pons contains two distinct populations of neurons: 1) lower motor neurons that project directly via the exiting CN VI nerve root to the ipsilateral lateral rectus, and 2) internuclear interneurons whose axons decussate and ascend within the contralateral medial longitudinal fasciculus (MLF) to innervate the contralateral medial rectus subnucleus of CN III. Therefore, a lesion of the abducens NUCLEUS causes a complete conjugate horizontal gaze palsy toward the side of the lesion (neither eye can look ipsilaterally). In contrast, a lesion of the exiting abducens NERVE ROOT affects only the peripheral motor axons to the lateral rectus, resulting in isolated failure of ipsilateral abduction with preserved contralateral adduction.

**High-Yield Pearl**: An abducens nucleus lesion causes a conjugate horizontal gaze palsy; an abducens nerve root lesion causes isolated ipsilateral abduction failure.

---

## Question 5 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q25`

A 50-year-old patient presents with a visual field defect caused by an aneurysm compressing the uncrossed fibers on the lateral aspect of the optic chiasm on the right side. What visual field loss occurs from damaging these uncrossed temporal retinal fibers on the right?

- **(A)** Loss of the nasal visual field in the right eye ✅
- **(B)** Loss of the temporal visual field in the right eye
- **(C)** Loss of the temporal visual field in both eyes
- **(D)** Total blindness in the left eye
- **(E)** Right upper quadrantanopia

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Uncrossed fibers on the lateral aspect of the optic chiasm originate from the temporal hemiretina of the ipsilateral (right) eye. The temporal hemiretina receives light from the nasal visual field of that same eye. Damage to right lateral chiasm uncrossed fibers causes loss of the nasal visual field in the right eye. Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) misidentify pathway laterality and fields.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Loss of the nasal visual field in the right eye

---

## Question 6 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q41`

A 70-year-old woman is brought to the clinic due to sudden onset of mild vertigo, nausea, and unsteady gait. Neurological exam reveals dysmetria on left arm movement, horizontal nystagmus that beats to the left and does NOT suppress with visual fixation, and no hearing loss. MRI confirms a small cerebellar stroke. Which of the following features best distinguishes central vertigo from peripheral vertigo?

- **(A)** Presence of visual suppression of nystagmus in central vertigo
- **(B)** Association with low-frequency sensorineural hearing loss in central vertigo
- **(C)** Non-fatigable nystagmus and presence of additional central neurological signs ✅
- **(D)** Complete resolution of vertigo within 30 seconds of head position change
- **(E)** Positive Dix-Hallpike test with a latency of 10 seconds

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: Central vertigo features non-fatigable, direction-changing or vertical nystagmus, absence of visual suppression, and central brainstem/cerebellar signs (dysmetria, ataxia). Peripheral vertigo features fatigable nystagmus, latency, and visual suppression. Option (A) occurs in peripheral vertigo; Option (B) occurs in peripheral Ménière disease; Option (D) describes peripheral BPPV; Option (E) describes peripheral BPPV.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Non-fatigable nystagmus and presence of additional central neurological signs

---

## Question 7 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q25`

A 29-year-old woman is evaluated for hearing loss in her left ear. A Rinne test is performed using a 512 Hz tuning fork: bone conduction is heard longer and louder than air conduction when placed on the left mastoid process (negative Rinne). When the tuning fork is placed on the midline forehead (Weber test), the sound is heard loudest in the left ear. What type of hearing loss is present in the left ear, and what is a classic middle ear cause?

- **(A)** Sensorineural hearing loss; presbycusis
- **(B)** Conductive hearing loss; otosclerosis ✅
- **(C)** Sensorineural hearing loss; vestibular schwannoma
- **(D)** Conductive hearing loss; labyrinthitis
- **(E)** Central auditory processing loss; cortical stroke

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Negative Rinne on the left (Bone Conduction > Air Conduction) indicates conductive hearing loss in the left ear because bone vibration bypasses middle ear mechanical obstruction. Weber lateralizes to the affected (left) ear. Otosclerosis (stapes footplate fixation) is a classic cause of conductive hearing loss in young adults. Option (A) causes positive Rinne; Option (C) causes sensorineural loss; Option (D) causes sensorineural loss; Option (E) is central.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Conductive hearing loss; otosclerosis

---

## Question 8 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q10`

A 52-year-old accountant suffers an ischemic stroke involving the caudal pons on the left side. Examination reveals an inability to abduct the left eye. Additionally, the patient exhibits weakness of both the upper and lower facial muscles on the left side, including an inability to close the left eye or wrinkle the left forehead. Which combination of neural structures in the caudal pons is involved in this patient's lesion?

- **(A)** Left abducens nucleus and left facial nerve motor nucleus/fibers ✅
- **(B)** Left oculomotor nucleus and right corticobulbar tract
- **(C)** Left trochlear nucleus and left facial motor nucleus
- **(D)** Right abducens nucleus and left corticospinal tract
- **(E)** Left Edinger-Westphal nucleus and left trigeminal motor nucleus

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The caudal pons houses the motor nucleus of CN VI (abducens) and the motor nucleus of CN VII (facial). Exiting facial nerve fibers loop around the abducens nucleus forming the facial colliculus. A lesion in the left caudal pons damages the left abducens nucleus/nerve (inability to abduct the left eye) and left facial motor nucleus/nerve (total, lower motor neuron paralysis of ipsilateral upper and lower facial muscles). Option (B) involves midbrain CN III; Option (C) involves midbrain CN IV; Option (D) involves right side; Option (E) involves Edinger-Westphal in midbrain.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Left abducens nucleus and left facial nerve motor nucleus/fibers

---

## Question 9 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q7`

A 29-year-old woman is brought to the emergency clinic following a motor vehicle collision. Neurological examination reveals complete loss of voluntary movement, loss of pain and temperature sensation below the level of the T10 dermatome, and preserved fine touch, vibration, and proprioception bilaterally. Deep tendon reflexes in the lower extremities are initially absent but become hyperactive four weeks later with bilateral clonus. Which of the following arterial territories was most likely compromised in this patient?

- **(A)** Brown-Séquard syndrome involving unilateral spinal cord hemisection
- **(B)** Anterior spinal artery syndrome causing bilateral ventral cord ischemia ✅
- **(C)** Central cord syndrome affecting crossing spinothalamic commissural fibers
- **(D)** Tabes dorsalis causing bilateral demyelination of posterior dorsal columns
- **(E)** Subacute combined degeneration affecting dorsal and lateral columns

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The anterior spinal artery (ASA) supplies the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord, including the lateral corticospinal tracts, anterolateral system (spinothalamic tracts), and ventral horns bilaterally. ASA occlusion causes Anterior Cord Syndrome, resulting in bilateral motor paralysis and bilateral loss of pain and temperature sensation below the lesion level, with complete preservation of the dorsal columns (fine touch, vibration, proprioception) supplied by posterior spinal arteries. Option (A) causes Posterior Cord Syndrome; Option (C) produces internal capsule hemiplegia; Option (D) causes lateral medullary syndrome; Option (E) causes contralateral leg weakness without anterior cord syndrome.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Anterior spinal artery

---

## Question 10 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q15`

A 42-year-old male presents with difficulty walking down stairs. He reports that when looking down at the steps, he experiences vertical diplopia. To compensate and eliminate the double vision, he tilts his head away from the affected side (head tilt to the left). Physical examination demonstrates weakness of depression when the right eye is in an adducted position. Which cranial nerve is injured?

- **(A)** Right oculomotor nerve exiting the interpeduncular fossa
- **(B)** Right trochlear nerve exiting the dorsal midbrain tegmentum ✅
- **(C)** Right abducens nerve exiting the pontomedullary junction
- **(D)** Left trochlear nerve exiting the dorsal midbrain tegmentum
- **(E)** Right trigeminal nerve exiting the mid-pontine tegmentum

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The trochlear nerve (CN IV) innervates the superior oblique muscle, which depresses, intorts, and abducts the eye; its depressive action is greatest when the eye is in an adducted position. Patients with a trochlear nerve palsy experience vertical diplopia that is most pronounced when looking down and inward (e.g., reading a book or walking downstairs). To eliminate the diplopia, patients characteristically tilt their head AWAY from the affected side (contralateral head tilt, here tilting to the left for a right CN IV palsy), which minimizes the need for intorsion by the paralyzed superior oblique muscle.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Trochlear nerve (CN IV) palsy causes vertical diplopia when walking downstairs and a compensatory head tilt AWAY from the affected side.

---

## Question 11 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q13`

An investigator traces the reflex loop for the pupillary light reflex. Light shone into the retina stimulates retinal ganglion cell axons that travel via the optic nerve, optic chiasm, and optic tract. Where do these reflex fibers bypass the LGN to synapse in the rostral midbrain?

- **(A)** Superior colliculus brachium projecting to the pretectal nucleus ✅
- **(B)** Inferior colliculus brachium projecting to the medial geniculate nucleus
- **(C)** Paramedian pontine reticular formation projecting to the abducens nucleus
- **(D)** Hypothalamic projection fibers entering the ciliospinal center
- **(E)** Red nucleus rubrospinal projections entering the rostral spinal cord

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: In the pupillary light reflex, visual sensory axons originating from retinal ganglion cells travel through the optic nerve, optic chiasm, and optic tract. Before reaching the lateral geniculate nucleus (LGN), the reflex fibers leave the optic tract via the brachium of the superior colliculus to synapse in the pretectal nucleus of the rostral midbrain. Interneurons from each pretectal nucleus then project bilaterally to both the ipsilateral and contralateral Edinger-Westphal nuclei (crossing fibers traverse the posterior commissure), establishing the anatomical basis for the consensual light reflex.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Afferent pupillary light reflex fibers bypass the LGN and travel via the brachium of the superior colliculus to synapse in the pretectal nucleus.

---

## Question 12 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q35`

A 70-year-old male with a history of dream enactment behavior (REM sleep behavior disorder) presents with a rest tremor and bradykinesia. Which neurodegenerative disease entity is strongly associated with REM sleep behavior disorder?

- **(A)** Huntington disease with trinucleotide CAG repeat expansion
- **(B)** Lewy body disease with pathological alpha-synuclein aggregation ✅
- **(C)** Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis with superoxide dismutase mutation
- **(D)** Multiple sclerosis with oligoclonal immunoglobulin bands in CSF
- **(E)** Alzheimer dementia with hyperphosphorylated tau neurofibrillary tangles

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: REM sleep behavior disorder (loss of REM muscle atonia with dream enactment) is strongly associated with neurodegenerative alpha-synucleinopathies, including Parkinson disease and Dementia with Lewy Bodies. Options (A), (C), (D), and (E) are not alpha-synucleinopathies.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Lewy body disease / Parkinson disease

---

## Question 13 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q16`

A 50-year-old female presents with a lesion interrupting the posterior commissure in the dorsal midbrain. Which ocular movement pathway is specifically disrupted by damaging crossing fibers in the posterior commissure?

- **(A)** Horizontal conjugate gaze pathway mediated by the PPRF and MLF
- **(B)** Vertical conjugate gaze pathway coordinated by the pretectal area ✅
- **(C)** Oculosympathetic pupillary dilation pathway through the reticular formation
- **(D)** Primary auditory transmission pathway through the lateral lemniscus
- **(E)** Lateral vestibulospinal tract modulating lower extremity extensor tone

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The posterior commissure is a vital cross-linking tract located in the dorsal midbrain tegmentum immediately rostral to the superior colliculus. It carries decussating fibers essential for vertical conjugate gaze (linking the bilateral rostral interstitial nuclei of the MLF and the interstitial nuclei of Cajal) as well as consensual pupillary light reflex fibers linking pretectal neurons to contralateral Edinger-Westphal nuclei. Interruption of the posterior commissure selectively disrupts vertical conjugate gaze (particularly upward vertical gaze, as seen in Parinaud syndrome) while leaving horizontal gaze completely intact.

**High-Yield Pearl**: The posterior commissure coordinates vertical conjugate gaze; its disruption selectively paralyzes upward gaze while sparing horizontal gaze.

---

## Question 14 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q24`

A neuroanatomy student examines a cross-section of the thalamus. The lateral geniculate nucleus (LGN) is identified as the thalamic relay center for vision. LGN neurons receive third-order axons from retinal ganglion cells via the optic tract and project fourth-order axons to the primary visual cortex via which fiber bundle?

- **(A)** Lateral lemniscus auditory transmission pathway
- **(B)** Geniculocalcarine optic radiation projection pathway ✅
- **(C)** Medial longitudinal fasciculus conjugate gaze tract
- **(D)** Internal capsule anterior limb frontopontine tract
- **(E)** Trapezoid body decussating auditory relay fibers

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Fourth-order neurons in the LGN project axons via the optic radiations (geniculocalcarine tract)—divided into temporal Meyer's loop and parietal dorsal loop—through the subcortical white matter to the primary visual cortex (V1) surrounding the calcarine sulcus. Lateral lemniscus (A) is auditory; MLF (C) coordinates eye movements; Internal capsule anterior limb (D) carries frontopontine fibers; Trapezoid body (E) decussates auditory fibers.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Optic radiations (geniculocalcarine tract)

---

## Question 15 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q1`

A 24-year-old medical student participates in a sleep physiology study. Electroencephalogram (EEG) electrodes are placed on her scalp while she lies comfortably in a quiet, dark room with her eyes closed, remaining relaxed but awake. Which of the following EEG wave patterns will predominate during this relaxed, awake state with eyes closed?

- **(A)** High-frequency, low-voltage desynchronized beta waves
- **(B)** Synchronized regular sinusoidal alpha waves ✅
- **(C)** Low-voltage, mixed-frequency irregular theta waves
- **(D)** Stage two sleep spindles and prominent K-complexes
- **(E)** High-voltage, slow synchronized delta waves

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: EEG frequency rhythms depend on arousal state. Awake, alert, active concentration displays beta waves (>13 Hz). Awake, relaxed state with eyes closed displays synchronized alpha waves (8–12 Hz), predominantly over occipital/parietal lobes. Option (A) occurs with eyes open/alert concentration; Option (C) occurs in N1 sleep; Option (D) occurs in N2 sleep; Option (E) occurs in N3 slow-wave sleep.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Synchronized alpha waves (8–12 Hz)

---

## Question 16 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q50`

A 52-year-old male with a history of recurrent middle ear infections presents with progressive, painless conductive hearing loss in his left ear. Otoscopic exam reveals a pearly-white mass behind the tympanic membrane with erosion of the incus. What is a serious intracranial complication of an untreated, expanding cholesteatoma?

- **(A)** Temporal lobe or epidural abscess secondary to tegmental bone erosion ✅
- **(B)** Cavernous sinus thrombosis secondary to facial venous thrombophlebitis
- **(C)** Pituitary apoplexy secondary to intrasellar ischemic hemorrhage
- **(D)** Subclavian steal syndrome secondary to proximal subclavian stenosis
- **(E)** Carotid-cavernous fistula secondary to basal skull trauma

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Expanding cholesteatomas release lytic enzymes that erode surrounding bone. Superior erosion through the roof of the middle ear cavity (tegmen tympani) allows infection to spread into the middle cranial fossa, causing temporal lobe abscess, epidural abscess, or meningitis. Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) do not occur from temporal bone erosion.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Temporal lobe abscess or epidural abscess due to erosion of the tegmental bone

---

## Question 17 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q30`

A 22-year-old patient presents to the emergency department following an acute overdose of an over-the-counter medication. The patient complains of high-pitched ringing in both ears (tinnitus) and nausea. Which of the following ototoxic substances was most likely ingested?

- **(A)** Salicylates such as high-dose aspirin ✅
- **(B)** Macrolides such as erythromycin base
- **(C)** Penicillins such as oral amoxicillin
- **(D)** Cephalosporins such as cephalexin
- **(E)** Antifungals such as fluconazole

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: High-dose salicylates (aspirin) cause reversible high-pitched tinnitus and mild sensorineural hearing loss by inhibiting outer hair cell motor function (prestin) and altering cochlear blood flow. Salicylates also stimulate medullary respiratory centers. Option (B) causes hepatotoxicity; Option (C) causes anticholinergic signs; Option (D) causes nephrotoxicity/ulcers; Option (E) causes pseudotumor cerebri.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Salicylates (Aspirin)

---

## Question 18 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q51`

A 61-year-old female presents with balance impairment. Neurological examination reveals lower extremity hyperreflexia (3+ patellar, 3+ Achilles), velocity-dependent spasticity in both legs, and bilateral ankle clonus. Plantar response shows upgoing toes bilaterally. Upper extremity reflexes and strength are entirely normal. A lesion at which level of the neuraxis best accounts for these findings?

- **(A)** Thoracic spinal cord lateral corticospinal tracts bilaterally ✅
- **(B)** Cervical spinal cord anterior horn cells
- **(C)** Caudal medulla pyramids bilaterally
- **(D)** Bilateral precentral gyrus arm motor representation
- **(E)** Peripheral nerves of the lumbosacral plexus

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Bilateral lower extremity hyperreflexia, ankle clonus, spasticity, and extensor plantar responses represent upper motor neuron deficits isolated to both legs. Normal upper extremity strength and reflexes exclude lesions in the cervical cord, brainstem, or cerebral hemispheres. Thus, the lesion is in the thoracic spinal cord lateral corticospinal tracts bilaterally (below cervical enlargement, above lumbar enlargement). Option (B) causes LMN arm weakness; Option (C) causes spastic quadriplegia involving arms and legs; Option (D) affects arms; Option (E) causes LMN leg flaccidity.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Thoracic spinal cord lateral corticospinal tracts bilaterally

---

## Question 19 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q6`

An investigator studies the neurochemical control of circadian rhythms. Neurons in the master circadian pacemaker respond to light signals transmitted directly from melanopsin-containing retinal ganglion cells via the retinohypothalamic tract. What is the location of this primary circadian pacemaker in the brain?

- **(A)** Ventrolateral preoptic nucleus of the anterior hypothalamus
- **(B)** Suprachiasmatic nucleus of the anterior hypothalamus ✅
- **(C)** Lateral hypothalamic area containing orexinergic neurons
- **(D)** Arcuate nucleus regulating adenohypophyseal secretion
- **(E)** Mammillary bodies projecting through the mammillothalamic tract

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The suprachiasmatic nucleus (SCN) in the anterior hypothalamus above the optic chiasm is the master circadian pacemaker. Melanopsin-containing intrinsically photosensitive retinal ganglion cells (ipRGCs) project via the retinohypothalamic tract to entrain the SCN to external light-dark cycles. Option (A) is the sleep-promoting GABA center; Option (C) contains orexin neurons; Option (D) produces dopamine; Option (E) secretes melatonin downstream of SCN signals.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Suprachiasmatic nucleus (SCN) of the hypothalamus

---

## Question 20 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q22`

A 30-year-old male medical resident undergoes EEG testing during a call shift. While actively working on a complex patient chart, his EEG shows low-voltage, high-frequency beta waves (>13 Hz). What state of arousal is characterized by beta wave activity?

- **(A)** Deep slow-wave N3 sleep with delta rhythms
- **(B)** Awake, alert mental concentration with beta rhythms ✅
- **(C)** Drowsy, relaxed eyes-closed rest with alpha rhythms
- **(D)** Light stage N1 sleep with vertex sharp waves
- **(E)** Stage N2 sleep with sleep spindles and K-complexes

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Beta waves (>13 Hz, low-voltage) define an awake, alert state with active mental concentration or eyes open. Alpha waves (8–12 Hz) define relaxed wakefulness with eyes closed. Theta waves (4–7 Hz) define N1 sleep. Delta waves (<4 Hz) define N3 deep sleep. Options (A), (C), (D), and (E) misidentify EEG wave correlations.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Awake, alert, and active mental concentration

---

## Question 21 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q52`

A 35-year-old female complains of daytime fatigue. A full-night sleep study is performed. The report describes sleep architecture metrics. Which sleep stage is characterized by slow delta waves on EEG, represents the deepest restorative sleep, and features the lowest levels of blood pressure, heart rate, and respiration?

- **(A)** Stage 3 NREM ✅
- **(B)** Stage 1 NREM
- **(C)** Stage 2 NREM
- **(D)** REM sleep
- **(E)** Awake state

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Stage 3 NREM (slow-wave sleep) is characterized by slow delta waves on EEG, represents the deepest restorative sleep, features peak growth hormone secretion, and exhibits lowest baseline blood pressure, heart rate, and respiration. Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) do not define slow-wave deep sleep.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Stage 3 NREM

---

## Question 22 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q19`

A 68-year-old male is brought to the neurology clinic by his wife. She reports that for the past two years, her husband has been violently flailing his arms, punching, and kicking in his sleep, often shouting at imaginary intruders. Last week, he accidentally struck his wife while dreaming that he was defending himself. Polysomnography reveals loss of normal somatic muscle atonia during REM sleep. What neurological condition is strongly associated with the long-term development of this parasomnia (REM sleep behavior disorder)?

- **(A)** Huntington chorea with caudate nucleus atrophy
- **(B)** Lewy body disease with alpha-synuclein inclusions ✅
- **(C)** Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis with motor neuron loss
- **(D)** Multiple sclerosis with periventricular demyelination
- **(E)** Alzheimer dementia with cortical amyloid plaques

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: REM Sleep Behavior Disorder (RBD) involves loss of normal somatic muscle atonia during REM sleep, causing patients to physically act out dreams. RBD is a major prodromal biomarker for alpha-synucleinopathies, specifically Parkinson disease, Dementia with Lewy Bodies, and Multiple System Atrophy. Options (A), (C), (D), and (E) are not primary alpha-synucleinopathies associated with RBD.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Lewy body disease / Parkinson disease

---

## Question 23 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q41`

An investigator analyzes visual acuity across the retina. Why does the fovea centralis achieve the highest visual acuity in the retina?

- **(A)** High concentration of rods with massive convergence onto single ganglion cells
- **(B)** Exclusively cones with one-to-one wiring to bipolar cells and pushed aside layers ✅
- **(C)** Dense network of overlying retinal capillaries providing intense aerobic perfusion
- **(D)** High concentration of melanopsin ganglion cells driving circadian photoentrainment
- **(E)** Thick overlying extracellular gelatinous matrix containing mineralized statoconia

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Maximum visual acuity at the fovea centralis is achieved because it contains exclusively cone photoreceptors tightly packed with a 1:1 synaptic ratio to bipolar and ganglion cells (no spatial convergence) and overlying neural layers/capillaries are displaced laterally to minimize light scattering. Rods (A) convergence lowers acuity; Capillaries (C) scatter light; ipRGCs (D) mediate circadian lighting; Otoliths (E) are vestibular.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Exclusively cone photoreceptors with 1:1 wiring to bipolar and ganglion cells and minimal overlying cell layers

---

## Question 24 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q49`

A 70-year-old male presents with an acute stroke. Physical examination shows left-sided spastic body hemiplegia, left-sided lower face droop, and tongue deviation to the left on protrusion. Forehead wrinkling is intact bilaterally. No sensory loss is identified. Which specific vascular structure was most likely occluded?

- **(A)** Left middle cerebral artery cortical branches supplying the insular cortex
- **(B)** Right middle cerebral artery lenticulostriate arteries supplying internal capsule ✅
- **(C)** Right anterior cerebral artery branches supplying medial paracentral lobule
- **(D)** Basilar artery paramedian penetrating branches supplying the pontine base
- **(E)** Left posterior inferior cerebellar artery supplying lateral medullary tegmentum

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Penetrating lenticulostriate branches of the right MCA supply the posterior limb and genu of the right internal capsule. Ischemia interrupts uncrossed corticospinal fibers (causing left-sided body spastic hemiplegia) and corticobulbar fibers (causing left lower facial droop and left tongue deviation upon protrusion), with upper forehead sparing. Option (A) causes right-sided body/face weakness; Option (C) causes bilateral paraplegia; Option (D) causes lateral medullary syndrome; Option (E) causes contralateral leg weakness.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right middle cerebral artery (penetrating lenticulostriate branches)

---

## Question 25 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q52`

A 62-year-old male presents with sudden onset of vertigo, hoarseness, dysphagia, and left-sided Horner syndrome (ptosis, miosis, anhidrosis). Neurological exam shows loss of pain and temperature sensation on the left side of his face and right side of his body. Hearing is completely normal. An ischemic stroke involving the lateral medulla (Wallenberg syndrome) is diagnosed. Which artery was occluded?

- **(A)** Posterior inferior cerebellar artery (PICA) ✅
- **(B)** Anterior cerebral artery (ACA)
- **(C)** Middle cerebral artery (MCA)
- **(D)** Internal carotid artery (ICA)
- **(E)** Anterior spinal artery (ASA)

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Lateral medullary syndrome (Wallenberg syndrome) results from occlusion of PICA, supplying the dorsolateral medulla. Damaged structures include vestibular nuclei (vertigo, nystagmus), nucleus ambiguus (dysphagia, hoarseness, palatal droop), sympathetic fibers (ipsilateral Horner syndrome), spinal trigeminal nucleus (ipsilateral facial pain/temp loss), and anterolateral system (contralateral body pain/temp loss). Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) do not cause lateral medullary syndrome.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Posterior inferior cerebellar artery (PICA)

---

## Question 26 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q20`

A 22-year-old medical resident evaluates extraocular muscle innervation. Which extraocular muscle is innervated by the trochlear nerve (CN IV), and what is its primary physiological action when the eye is adducted?

- **(A)** Lateral rectus muscle causing horizontal abduction of the eye
- **(B)** Superior oblique muscle causing depression when adducted and intorsion ✅
- **(C)** Inferior oblique muscle causing elevation when adducted and extorsion
- **(D)** Medial rectus muscle causing horizontal adduction of the eye
- **(E)** Superior rectus muscle causing elevation when abducted and intorsion

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Extraocular motor innervation follows the classic mnemonic SO4 LR6 EE3 (Superior Oblique by CN IV, Lateral Rectus by CN VI, and Everyone Else by CN III). The trochlear nerve (CN IV) innervates exclusively the superior oblique muscle. Because the tendon of the superior oblique passes through the fibrous trochlea before inserting on the posterolateral sclera, its primary actions are intorsion (internal rotation) and depression of the eye when it is in an adducted (turned inward) position.

**High-Yield Pearl**: The trochlear nerve (CN IV) innervates exclusively the superior oblique muscle, which depresses the adducted eye and intorts the globe.

## Question 27 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q47`

An anatomical study traces the geniculocalcarine tract. Axons forming the dorsal loop of optic radiations pass through which cerebral lobe on their way to the cuneus gyrus?

- **(A)** Temporal lobe
- **(B)** Parietal lobe ✅
- **(C)** Frontal lobe
- **(D)** Insular lobe
- **(E)** Limbic lobe

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The geniculocalcarine tract (optic radiations) divides into two bundles: the superior optic radiations (dorsal loop) travel through the parietal lobe to the cuneus gyrus, while the inferior optic radiations (Meyer loop) travel through the temporal lobe to the lingual gyrus. Options (A), (C), (D), and (E) misidentify the lobe for dorsal loop fibers.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Parietal lobe

---

## Question 28 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q38`

A 52-year-old patient undergoes an ocular examination. The clinician evaluates aqueous humor flow. Aqueous humor passes from the posterior chamber into the anterior chamber through which opening?

- **(A)** Pupil opening between the iris margins ✅
- **(B)** Canal of Schlemm at the iridocorneal angle
- **(C)** Optic disc where ganglion cell axons exit
- **(D)** Cornea transmitting anterior incident light
- **(E)** Fovea centralis containing cone receptors

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Aqueous humor is secreted by the ciliary body into the posterior chamber, flows through the pupil into the anterior chamber, and drains via the trabecular meshwork into the Canal of Schlemm. Canal of Schlemm (B) drains fluid out of the anterior chamber; Optic disc (C), cornea (D), and fovea (E) do not form the flow aperture between posterior and anterior chambers.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Pupil

## Question 29 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q29`

A 35-year-old male with narcolepsy is prescribed modafinil to treat severe daytime sleepiness. What is the primary mechanism of action of modafinil in promoting wakefulness?

- **(A)** Direct agonist at GABAA receptor chloride channels
- **(B)** Non-amphetamine wake-promoting agent that enhances wakefulness centers ✅
- **(C)** Antagonist at MT1 and MT2 melatonin receptors
- **(D)** Irreversible inhibitor of acetylcholinesterase
- **(E)** Selective histamine H1 receptor antagonist

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Modafinil is a first-line non-amphetamine wake-promoting agent used in narcolepsy to treat excessive daytime sleepiness by modulating wake-promoting brain centers and monoamine dynamics. Option (A) describes GABAA hypnotics; Option (C) describes ramelteon antagonists; Option (D) describes cholinesterase inhibitors; Option (E) describes antihistamines.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Non-amphetamine wake-promoting agent that enhances wakefulness centers

## Question 30 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q23`

A 35-year-old female presents with hyperopia (farsightedness). Refractive evaluation shows that light rays entering the eye are focused behind the retina when viewing near objects. What structural problem causes hyperopia?

- **(A)** Eye axial length too short ✅
- **(B)** Eye axial length too long
- **(C)** Corneal curvature irregularity
- **(D)** Lens opacity from denatured proteins
- **(E)** Failure of the optic fissure to close

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Hyperopia (farsightedness) occurs when the axial length of the eyeball is structurally too short relative to its refractive power, causing light rays from nearby objects to focus behind the retina. Eye too long (B) causes myopia; Corneal irregularity (C) causes astigmatism; Lens opacity (D) causes cataracts; Optic fissure defect (E) causes coloboma.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Eye axial length too short

## Question 31 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q38`

A 30-year-old female presents with anisocoria (unequal pupil sizes). In room light, her left pupil is larger than her right pupil. When the room lights are dimmed, the degree of anisocoria increases significantly (the right pupil fails to dilate in darkness). Which autonomic pathway is impaired, and in which eye?

- **(A)** Parasympathetic defect in the left eye where anisocoria increases in bright light ✅
- **(B)** Sympathetic defect in the right eye where anisocoria increases in dim darkness
- **(C)** Parasympathetic defect in the right eye where anisocoria increases in darkness
- **(D)** Sympathetic defect in the left eye where anisocoria increases in bright light
- **(E)** Bilateral complete transection of ascending optic nerve sensory projections

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Evaluation of anisocoria (asymmetric pupil size) requires comparing the pupil sizes in both bright light and dim darkness. When anisocoria is GREATER IN BRIGHT LIGHT, the abnormal pupil is the larger one because it fails to constrict appropriately, indicating an impaired parasympathetic pupillomotor pathway (e.g., CN III compression or Adie tonic pupil). Conversely, when anisocoria is GREATER IN DIM DARKNESS, the abnormal pupil is the smaller one because it fails to dilate in the dark, indicating an impaired sympathetic pathway (Horner syndrome). Here, the left pupil is larger in room light, and if anisocoria worsens in light, the lesion is a left parasympathetic defect.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Anisocoria greater in bright light indicates a parasympathetic defect in the larger pupil; anisocoria greater in dim darkness indicates a sympathetic defect in the smaller pupil.

---

## Question 32 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q13`

An anatomical study evaluates the dorsal midbrain. The superior colliculi are identified on the tectal plate. What is the primary physiological function of the superior colliculi in response to visual stimuli?

- **(A)** Transduction of high-frequency acoustic pitch
- **(B)** Direct relay of visual signals to the medial geniculate nucleus
- **(C)** Coordination of head and eye turning reflexes toward visual stimuli ✅
- **(D)** Secretion of aqueous humor into the posterior chamber
- **(E)** Processing of complex speech syntax

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: The superior colliculi in the rostral midbrain tectum receive visual projections and mediate visual reflexes, specifically coordinating reflex head and eye movement orienting toward visual stimuli in the environment. Inferior colliculi (A, E) mediate auditory reflexes. MGN (B) is thalamic. Ciliary body (D) secretes fluid.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Coordination of head and eye turning reflexes toward visual stimuli

---

## Question 33 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q19`

A clinician performs a pupillary examination on a patient with a left CN III palsy. When light is shone into the healthy right eye, what pattern of pupillary response will be observed?

- **(A)** Direct right pupil constriction and absent consensual left pupil constriction ✅
- **(B)** Absent direct right pupil constriction and intact consensual left pupil constriction
- **(C)** Complete absence of pupillary constriction in both pupils upon illumination
- **(D)** Prompt direct and consensual pupillary constriction in both pupils bilaterally
- **(E)** Active pupillary dilation mediated by dilatator pupillae in the right eye

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The patient has a left oculomotor nerve (CN III) palsy, which damages the efferent parasympathetic fibers supplying the left sphincter pupillae muscle. When light is shone into the healthy right eye, the afferent signal travels normally through the right optic nerve (CN II) to both pretectal nuclei and bilaterally to both Edinger-Westphal nuclei. The right oculomotor nerve (CN III) is intact, so the right pupil constricts promptly (intact direct response). However, the left oculomotor nerve is injured, so parasympathetic motor impulses cannot reach the left sphincter pupillae, resulting in absent left pupil constriction (absent consensual response).

**High-Yield Pearl**: In a unilateral efferent CN III lesion, light in the contralateral healthy eye elicits an intact direct response but an absent consensual response in the paretic eye.

---

## Question 34 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q11`

A 50-year-old male cancer patient undergoing chemotherapy with high-dose cisplatin reports new-onset bilateral ringing in his ears (tinnitus) and difficulty hearing high-pitched sounds. Which of the following drug classes is also well-recognized for its ototoxic potential targeting cochlear hair cells?

- **(A)** Macrolide antibiotics such as azithromycin and clarithromycin
- **(B)** Aminoglycoside antibiotics such as gentamicin and tobramycin ✅
- **(C)** Fluoroquinolone antibiotics such as ciprofloxacin and levofloxacin
- **(D)** Beta-lactam antibiotics such as ceftriaxone and ampicillin
- **(E)** Tetracycline antibiotics such as doxycycline and minocycline

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Ototoxic drugs damage cochlear hair cells and vestibular sensory epithelia, causing sensorineural hearing loss, tinnitus, and vertigo. Major ototoxic classes include aminoglycoside antibiotics, platinum chemotherapeutics (cisplatin), loop diuretics (furosemide), and salicylates. Option (A) is non-ototoxic; Option (C) is non-ototoxic; Option (D) is non-ototoxic; Option (E) is non-ototoxic.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Aminoglycosides (e.g., gentamicin)

---

## Question 35 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q43`

A 56-year-old female presents with difficulty chewing. Examination reveals that when she opens her jaw, it deviates to the right. Left-sided jaw opening is normal. Facial sensation to light touch and pinprick is completely normal across all three trigeminal divisions on both sides of the face. Corneal reflexes are present bilaterally. What is the location of the lesion?

- **(A)** Left trigeminal motor nucleus
- **(B)** Right trigeminal motor nucleus or nerve root ✅
- **(C)** Left precentral gyrus motor cortex
- **(D)** Right corticobulbar tract in the genu of the internal capsule
- **(E)** Nucleus ambiguus on the right side

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The lateral pterygoid muscle opens the jaw and protrudes it toward the opposite side. An LMN lesion of the right trigeminal motor nucleus or nerve root (CN V) in the mid-pons weakens the right lateral pterygoid, causing the jaw to deviate toward the weak/lesioned side (right) upon opening. Intact facial sensation confirms an isolated motor root/nucleus lesion. Option (A) causes jaw deviation to the left; Option (C) and (D) do not cause functional jaw deviation due to bilateral corticobulbar input; Option (E) affects soft palate.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right trigeminal motor nucleus or nerve root

---

## Question 36 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q20`

A 52-year-old obese male with a history of hypertension presents with excessive daytime sleepiness, morning headaches, and loud, irregular snoring punctuated by gasping episodes during the night reported by his wife. Polysomnography demonstrates recurrent collapse of the pharyngeal airway during sleep, resulting in respiratory apneas and arterial desaturations. What is the primary first-line treatment for this patient's condition (Obstructive Sleep Apnea)?

- **(A)** Modafinil wakefulness-promoting therapy at morning awakening
- **(B)** Nocturnal continuous positive airway pressure via nasal mask ✅
- **(C)** Oral zolpidem non-benzodiazepine hypnotic therapy at bedtime
- **(D)** Permanent tracheostomy bypass surgery below the cricoid ring
- **(E)** Bilateral transcutaneous phrenic nerve pacemaker stimulation

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) involves recurrent pharyngeal airway collapse during sleep, producing apneas, oxyhemoglobin desaturations, and daytime fatigue. First-line therapy is Continuous Positive Airway Pressure (CPAP), which acts as a pneumatic splint to hold the pharyngeal airway open. Option (A) does not fix mechanical airway collapse; Option (C) sedatives worsen OSA; Option (D) is reserved for life-threatening refractory cases; Option (E) is for central sleep apnea.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP)

---

## Question 37 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q50`

A 60-year-old male with chronic insomnia is prescribed ramelteon. What is the primary mechanism of action of ramelteon?

- **(A)** Agonist at central melatonin receptors ✅
- **(B)** Positive allosteric modulator of GABA-A receptors
- **(C)** Antagonist at orexin receptors
- **(D)** Antagonist at histamine H1 receptors
- **(E)** Inhibitor of monoamine oxidase

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Ramelteon is a selective agonist at MT1 and MT2 melatonin receptors in the suprachiasmatic nucleus, mimicking endogenous melatonin to facilitate sleep onset. Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) misdescribe its mechanism.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Agonist at central melatonin receptors

## Question 38 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q28`

A 55-year-old male presents with progressive difficulty maintaining an upright head position. Examination reveals weakness in cervical neck muscles. Axons of the medial vestibulospinal tract (MVST), which project bilaterally down the cervical spinal cord to control head position, originate from which vestibular nucleus?

- **(A)** Lateral vestibular nucleus
- **(B)** Medial vestibular nucleus ✅
- **(C)** Superior vestibular nucleus
- **(D)** Inferior vestibular nucleus
- **(E)** Edinger-Westphal nucleus

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The medial vestibulospinal tract (MVST) originates in the medial vestibular nucleus and descends bilaterally through the MLF into the cervical spinal cord to innervate neck muscles, controlling head position. Option (A) gives rise to LVST; Option (C) projects to extraocular nuclei; Option (D) integrates cerebellar inputs; Option (E) provides parasympathetic pupillary control.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Medial vestibular nucleus

## Question 39 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q35`

A 59-year-old male presents following a localized stroke in the brainstem. On physical examination, he demonstrates weakness of the left lateral rectus muscle, causing medial deviation of the left eye at rest. He also presents with total paralysis of the left facial muscles, involving both the upper forehead and lower mouth. Which brainstem level is damaged?

- **(A)** Rostral midbrain
- **(B)** Caudal midbrain
- **(C)** Mid-pons
- **(D)** Caudal pons ✅
- **(E)** Rostral medulla

**Correct Answer**: **(D)**

**Explanation**: The caudal pons houses the abducens nucleus (CN VI) and facial motor nucleus (CN VII). A left caudal pontine lesion injures left CN VI (inability to abduct left eye, medial deviation at rest) and left CN VII (total LMN facial paralysis involving upper forehead and lower mouth). Option (A) houses CN III; Option (B) houses CN IV; Option (C) houses CN V motor nucleus; Option (E) houses CN XII and nucleus ambiguus.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Caudal pons

---

## Question 40 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q4`

A 7-year-old boy is brought to the clinic by his parents because he frequently walks around the house during the middle of the night without waking up. Polysomnography confirms that these sleepwalking episodes (somnambulism) occur during the deepest, restorative phase of slow-wave sleep. Which EEG wave pattern defines this specific sleep stage (N3)?

- **(A)** High-frequency, low-voltage beta rhythms
- **(B)** Synchronized regular posterior alpha rhythms
- **(C)** Variable low-amplitude irregular theta rhythms
- **(D)** High-amplitude, synchronized slow delta rhythms ✅
- **(E)** Sawtooth waves with rapid conjugate eye movements

**Correct Answer**: **(D)**

**Explanation**: N3 (Stage 3 NREM), or slow-wave sleep (SWS), is defined on EEG by low-frequency, high-voltage delta waves (<4 Hz). N3 is the deepest restorative sleep stage, featuring lowest heart rate/blood pressure and peak growth hormone release. NREM parasomnias—somnambulism (sleepwalking), night terrors, and nocturnal enuresis—occur during N3 sleep. Option (A) defines beta waves; Option (B) defines alpha rhythm; Option (C) defines N2 sleep; Option (E) defines REM sleep.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Low-frequency, high-voltage delta waves (<4 Hz)

---

## Question 41 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q47`

A 22-year-old female experiences brief paralysis upon falling asleep accompanied by terrifying dream-like visions. She is fully conscious during the episode but unable to move her arms or legs. What term describes the loss of voluntary motor movement on waking or falling asleep?

- **(A)** Sleep paralysis ✅
- **(B)** Cataplexy
- **(C)** Somnambulism
- **(D)** Night terror
- **(E)** Bruxism

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Sleep paralysis is the transient inability to perform voluntary movements or speak while falling asleep (hypnagogic) or awakening (hypnopompic), caused by persistent REM sleep muscle atonia during conscious wakefulness. Option (B) is emotion-triggered tone loss; Option (C) is sleepwalking; Option (D) is N3 terror awakening; Option (E) is teeth grinding.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Sleep paralysis

## Question 42 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q55`

A 62-year-old male presents with an ischemic stroke in the territory of the left posterior cerebral artery (PCA) involving both the cuneus and lingual gyri of the left occipital lobe, but sparing the occipital pole due to collateral MCA blood flow. What visual field defect is present?

- **(A)** Right homonymous hemianopia with macular sparing ✅
- **(B)** Complete right homonymous hemianopia without macular sparing
- **(C)** Bitemporal hemianopia
- **(D)** Right upper quadrantanopia without macular sparing
- **(E)** Monocular blindness in left eye

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: An ischemic stroke destroying both the cuneus and lingual gyri of the left primary visual cortex interrupts processing for the entire contralateral (right) visual hemifield, producing a right homonymous hemianopia. Because the occipital pole (macular cortex) receives dual blood supply and is spared by collateral MCA flow, macular vision is preserved (macular sparing). Option (B) lacks macular collateral flow; Option (C) involves chiasm; Option (D) involves lingual gyrus only; Option (E) involves optic nerve.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right homonymous hemianopia with macular sparing

## Question 43 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q42`

A 30-year-old man presents for a sleep evaluation. Polysomnography demonstrates normal sleep architecture. An investigator measures neurochemical factors that promote wakefulness. Which lateral hypothalamic neuropeptide promotes wakefulness by inhibiting neurons of the VLPO?

- **(A)** Orexin lateral hypothalamic neuropeptide ✅
- **(B)** Melatonin pineal gland indolamine hormone
- **(C)** Gamma-aminobutyric acid neurotransmitter
- **(D)** Leptin adipose-derived satiety hormone
- **(E)** Oxytocin paraventricular nonapeptide hormone

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Orexin (hypocretin) neurons in the lateral hypothalamus promote wakefulness and stabilize arousal by inhibiting sleep-promoting neurons in the ventrolateral preoptic nucleus (VLPO). Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) do not function as the hypothalamic wake-stabilizing neuropeptide.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Orexin (hypocretin)

## Question 44 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q33`

A 75-year-old male presents with a 2-day history of difficulty swallowing and a nasal voice. Examination shows absent movement of the soft palate on the right side when he says "Ah," causing the uvula to pull toward the left. The gag reflex is absent on the right. When he is asked to turn his head to the left, strength in the right sternocleidomastoid muscle is normal. Tongue extension is in the midline. Where is the lesion located?

- **(A)** Right nucleus ambiguus in the rostral medulla ✅
- **(B)** Left hypoglossal nucleus in the rostral medulla
- **(C)** Right spinal accessory nucleus in the cervical cord
- **(D)** Left trigeminal motor nucleus in the mid-pons
- **(E)** Bilateral facial motor nuclei in the caudal pons

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The nucleus ambiguus in the rostral medulla houses LMN cell bodies for CN IX and X, innervating soft palate and pharyngeal muscles. A right nucleus ambiguus lesion causes right soft palate weakness and drooping. Upon saying "Ah," the intact left levator veli palatini contracts, pulling the uvula toward the healthy (left) side, away from the lesion. Right gag reflex is lost. Intact SCM and tongue extension confirm isolated nucleus ambiguus damage. Option (B) causes tongue deviation; Option (C) causes shoulder/neck weakness; Option (D) causes jaw deviation; Option (E) causes facial diplegia.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right nucleus ambiguus in the rostral medulla

## Question 45 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q33`

An investigator traces the sympathetic pathway for pupillary dilation. Preganglionic sympathetic axons exit the spinal cord via ventral roots at C8–T2 and enter the sympathetic chain via which structures?

- **(A)** Gray rami communicantes distributed at all spinal cord levels
- **(B)** White rami communicantes restricted to spinal levels T1 through L2 ✅
- **(C)** Dorsal sensory roots entering the posterior horn of the spinal cord
- **(D)** Long ciliary nerves traversing the posterior sclera into the choroid
- **(E)** Chorda tympani nerve traveling through the infratemporal fossa

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Second-order preganglionic sympathetic cell bodies are located in the intermediolateral (IML) cell column of the spinal cord from C8 to T2 (ciliospinal center of Budge). The myelinated preganglionic sympathetic axons exit the spinal cord via ventral motor roots and enter the sympathetic paravertebral chain exclusively through white rami communicantes, which are anatomically restricted to thoracic and upper lumbar spinal segments (T1–L2). They then ascend through the cervical sympathetic chain without synapsing until they reach the superior cervical ganglion.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Preganglionic sympathetic fibers exit the spinal cord and enter the sympathetic chain exclusively via white rami communicantes (T1–L2).

## Question 46 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q24`

A clinician performs the vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR) test on a comatose patient by rapidly rotating the patient's head to the left side (doll's eye maneuver). In a patient with an intact brainstem, both eyes smoothly deviate toward the right to maintain visual fixation. Which brainstem fiber tract links the vestibular nuclei to the contralateral abducens nucleus and ipsilateral oculomotor nucleus to coordinate this reflex eye movement?

- **(A)** Lateral lemniscus auditory tract in the brainstem
- **(B)** Medial longitudinal fasciculus connecting gaze nuclei ✅
- **(C)** Medial lemniscus ascending epicritic pathway in medulla
- **(D)** Corticospinal tract descending in the cerebral peduncles
- **(E)** Spinothalamic tract carrying pain and thermal signals

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR) maintains visual fixation during head rotation. Head turn to the left stimulates the left vestibular nucleus, which sends decussating fibers ascending via the medial longitudinal fasciculus (MLF) to the right abducens nucleus (CN VI) and contralateral oculomotor nucleus (CN III) to coordinate compensatory eye movements. Option (A) is auditory; Option (C) is decussating auditory bundle; Option (D) carries rubro-olivary fibers; Option (E) carries voluntary motor drive.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Medial longitudinal fasciculus (MLF)

## Question 47 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q35`

A 45-year-old male presents with visual field loss. Testing shows complete blindness in the left eye (left monocular anopia), while the right eye's visual field is completely normal. Where is the lesion located?

- **(A)** Left optic nerve ✅
- **(B)** Optic chiasm
- **(C)** Right optic tract
- **(D)** Left temporal lobe
- **(E)** Right visual cortex

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Complete loss of vision restricted to one eye (left monocular anopia) indicates a lesion anterior to the optic chiasm in the left eye or left optic nerve. Lesions at or posterior to the optic chiasm produce visual field defects affecting both eyes simultaneously. Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) cause binocular visual field defects.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Left optic nerve

## Question 48 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q49`

An investigator traces projections from the Frontal Eye Fields (FEF). The FEFs generate voluntary rapid eye movements to explore the visual environment. What type of conjugate eye movement is driven by the FEFs?

- **(A)** Conjugate horizontal saccadic eye movements exploring the visual field ✅
- **(B)** Smooth pursuit conjugate eye movements tracking a slowly moving target
- **(C)** Vestibulo-ocular reflex stabilizing retinal images during head rotations
- **(D)** Accommodation vergence adjusting focal depth for nearby visual targets
- **(E)** Optokinetic nystagmus slow phase maintaining continuity of visual scenes

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The frontal eye fields (FEF, Brodmann area 8) in the posterior middle frontal gyrus are the primary cortical control centers for voluntary SACCADIC eye movements—rapid, ballistic conjugate movements that quickly shift the fovea to new objects of interest in the visual field. In contrast, SMOOTH PURSUIT eye movements (slow tracking of moving objects) are controlled by the parietal-occipital-temporal association cortex, while the VOR is mediated by the vestibular nuclei and flocculonodular lobe.

**High-Yield Pearl**: The frontal eye fields (FEF) drive voluntary rapid saccadic eye movements, whereas parieto-occipital cortex mediates smooth pursuit.

## Question 49 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q19`

A 52-year-old male presents with a 1-year history of progressive, left-sided hearing loss, subtle imbalance when walking, and constant high-pitched tinnitus in his left ear. Physical examination reveals a positive Rinne test bilaterally (AC > BC) and a Weber test that lateralizes to the right ear. Neurological exam shows mild sensory loss to light touch over the left cheek (CN V1/V2 distribution) and subtle left lower facial weakness. Magnetic resonance imaging reveals a 2.5 cm enhancing mass located in the left cerebellopontine angle. What cell type gives rise to this tumor?

- **(A)** Oligodendrocytes
- **(B)** Schwann cells ✅
- **(C)** Astrocytes
- **(D)** Ependymal cells
- **(E)** Microglial cells

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: A vestibular schwannoma (acoustic neuroma) arises from Schwann cells of the vestibular division of CN VIII in the cerebellopontine angle (CPA). Expanding tumors compress CN VIII (sensorineural hearing loss, tinnitus, imbalance), CN VII (facial weakness), and CN V (facial numbness). Option (A) myelinates CNS; Option (C) forms astrocytomas; Option (D) lines ventricles; Option (E) represents CNS macrophages.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Schwann cells

---

## Question 50 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q4`

A 55-year-old male presents with unstable gait and frequent falls. Neurological examination reveals bilateral lower extremity spasticity, hyperreflexia at the knees and ankles, and bilateral upgoing plantar reflexes. The examiner suspects damage to descending upper motor neuron pathways. Which of the following statements accurately characterizes the anatomical trajectory of the lateral corticospinal tract?

- **(A)** Axons originate in precentral gyrus, decussate in anterior commissure, and descend in ventral funiculus
- **(B)** Axons originate in postcentral gyrus, decussate in ventral midbrain peduncles, and descend in dorsal funiculus
- **(C)** Axons originate in primary motor cortex, decussate in medullary pyramids, and descend in lateral funiculus ✅
- **(D)** Axons originate in pontine reticular formation, decussate in rostral pons, and descend in dorsal funiculus
- **(E)** Axons originate in ventral spinal gray horn, decussate in caudal medulla, and ascend in lateral funiculus

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: The lateral corticospinal tract originates from pyramidal neurons in the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus), descends through the posterior limb of the internal capsule, forms the crus cerebri in the midbrain, passes through the basilar pons, and forms the medullary pyramids. Approximately 90% of these fibers cross the midline in the medullary pyramids (pyramidal decussation) to descend in the contralateral lateral funiculus of the spinal cord. Option (A) describes anterior corticospinal fibers; Option (B) mislocates origin and decussation; Option (D) describes reticulospinal tracts; Option (E) describes primary sensory/peripheral neurons.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Axons originate in the motor cortex, descend through the posterior limb of the internal capsule, decussate in the medullary pyramids, and descend in the lateral funiculus

## Question 51 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q30`

A 28-year-old patient complaining of difficulty falling asleep is prescribed zaleplon. How does zaleplon differ pharmacokinetically from zolpidem and eszopiclone?

- **(A)** Ultra-short elimination half-life that avoids next-day sedation ✅
- **(B)** Irreversible 24-hour blockade of hypothalamic orexin receptors
- **(C)** Marked respiratory depression via non-selective barbiturate action
- **(D)** Long-acting prodrug requiring sequential renal tubular conversion
- **(E)** Selective stimulation of tuberomammillary histamine neurotransmission

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Zaleplon is a non-benzodiazepine hypnotic with an ultrashort elimination half-life (~1 hour), allowing rapid sleep initiation without morning hangover effects or next-day psychomotor impairment. Option (B) describes suvorexant; Option (C) is false for Z-drugs; Option (D) is false; Option (E) is false.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: It has an extremely short elimination half-life, making it useful for sleep-onset insomnia without next-day grogginess

## Question 52 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q46`

A 29-year-old female presents with weakness in turning her head. Examination demonstrates that she cannot turn her head to the right against resistance, and her left shoulder droops with 3/5 strength in left shoulder elevation. Strength in the right shoulder and right head turning is normal. Tongue extension is in the midline. Where is the primary lesion?

- **(A)** Left spinal accessory nucleus and peripheral motor nerve fibers ✅
- **(B)** Left hypoglossal motor nucleus in the floor of the fourth ventricle
- **(C)** Right corticobulbar tract in the posterior limb of internal capsule
- **(D)** Right primary motor cortex within the precentral motor homunculus
- **(E)** Left ambiguus nucleus in the lateral medullary reticular formation

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Lower motor neurons of CN XI innervate the sternocleidomastoid (SCM) and trapezius. The left SCM turns the head to the right, and the left trapezius elevates the left shoulder. Damage to the left spinal accessory motor nucleus/nerve causes LMN weakness of the left trapezius (impaired left shoulder shrug) and left SCM (inability to turn head to the right against resistance). Option (B) impairs right shoulder shrug and head turning to the left; Option (C) causes complex/bilateral mild UMN effects; Option (D) causes tongue deviation; Option (E) causes uvular deviation.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Left spinal accessory motor nucleus / nerve (CN XI)

## Question 53 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q7`

A 32-year-old male presents with severe pain and discharge from his right ear after swimming in a lake. Otoscopic exam reveals an erythematous, edematous right external auditory canal with purulent exudate; the tympanic membrane is partially obscured but intact. A Weber tuning fork test at 512 Hz lateralizes to the right ear. A Rinne test on the right ear shows bone conduction is greater than air conduction (negative test). Which pathogen is the most common cause of this condition (otitis externa)?

- **(A)** Streptococcus pneumoniae
- **(B)** Pseudomonas aeruginosa ✅
- **(C)** Haemophilus influenzae
- **(D)** Moraxella catarrhalis
- **(E)** Epstein-Barr virus

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Acute otitis externa ("Swimmer's ear") presents with ear canal pain, edema, erythema, purulent discharge, and conductive hearing loss (Weber to affected ear, negative Rinne). Excess water breakdown of cerumen allows bacterial infection, most commonly caused by Pseudomonas aeruginosa (followed by S. aureus). Option (A) causes otitis media; Option (C) causes otitis media; Option (D) causes otitis media; Option (E) causes mononucleosis.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Pseudomonas aeruginosa

## Question 54 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q35`

A 24-year-old patient presents with double vision when looking down and inward. Examination shows a trochlear nerve (CN IV) palsy on the left side. Where do trochlear nerve axons exit the brainstem before decussating?

- **(A)** Ventral surface of the midbrain between the cerebral peduncles
- **(B)** Dorsal surface of the midbrain caudal to the inferior colliculi ✅
- **(C)** Lateral pontomedullary junction adjacent to the flocculus
- **(D)** Ventral medullary surface between the pyramid and olive
- **(E)** Pretectal region of the thalamus adjacent to the pineal gland

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The trochlear nerve (CN IV) possesses two unique neuroanatomical distinctions among all twelve cranial nerves: 1) it is the ONLY cranial nerve that exits from the DORSAL (posterior) surface of the brainstem (emerging just caudal to the inferior colliculi of the midbrain), and 2) its fibers completely decussate within the superior medullary velum before exiting, meaning the left trochlear nucleus gives rise to the right trochlear nerve. Because of its long intracranial course around the midbrain tegmentum to reach the cavernous sinus, it is particularly susceptible to traumatic shearing injury during head collisions.

**High-Yield Pearl**: The trochlear nerve (CN IV) is the only cranial nerve that exits from the dorsal brainstem and decussates completely before exiting.

---

## Question 55 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q12`

A 30-year-old musician presents with hyperacusis (extreme sensitivity to everyday sounds), reporting that ordinary noises sound painfully loud in his left ear. Physical examination reveals an inability to wrinkle his left forehead, close his left eye, or smile on the left side. Which middle ear muscle is paralyzed in this patient, and which cranial nerve provides its innervation?

- **(A)** Tensor tympani muscle; trigeminal nerve (CN V3)
- **(B)** Stapedius muscle; facial nerve (CN VII) ✅
- **(C)** Tensor veli palatini muscle; glossopharyngeal nerve (CN IX)
- **(D)** Stapedius muscle; oculomotor nerve (CN III)
- **(E)** Tensor tympani muscle; vagus nerve (CN X)

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The stapedius muscle is innervated by the stapedial branch of CN VII. It dampens stapes movement at the oval window. Facial nerve palsy (Bell palsy) paralyzes the stapedius, causing hyperacusis (ordinary sounds feel painful). Tensor tympani is innervated by CN V3. Option (A) misidentifies the muscle in facial palsy; Option (C) opens Eustachian tube; Option (D) misidentifies nerve; Option (E) misidentifies nerve.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Stapedius muscle; facial nerve (CN VII)

## Question 56 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q15`

A 58-year-old male with end-stage renal disease on chronic hemodialysis complains of an uncomfortable, creeping sensation deep in both legs whenever he lies down to sleep in the evening. The uncomfortable sensation is temporarily relieved by getting out of bed and walking around, but returns as soon as he rests, causing severe sleep disruption. Laboratory evaluation demonstrates serum ferritin of 15 ng/mL (iron deficiency). What is the diagnosis?

- **(A)** Obstructive sleep apnea with pharyngeal airway collapse
- **(B)** Narcolepsy type 1 with cataplexy and orexin deficiency
- **(C)** Restless legs syndrome with uncomfortable leg sensations at rest ✅
- **(D)** REM sleep behavior disorder with loss of motor atonia
- **(E)** Sleep terror parasomnia arising from slow-wave sleep

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: Restless Legs Syndrome (RLS) presents with an irresistible urge to move the legs accompanied by deep crawling dysesthesias, worsening during rest/evening, and temporarily relieved by movement. It is strongly associated with iron deficiency (low serum ferritin) and renal disease. Option (A) involves acting out dreams during REM; Option (B) involves upper airway apneas; Option (D) involves daytime sleepiness without cataplexy; Option (E) involves involuntary leg jerks during sleep.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Restless legs syndrome (RLS)

## Question 57 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q27`

A 48-year-old male with a history of malnutrition presents with night blindness (nyctalopia) and dry eyes. Deficiency of which essential vitamin impairs the synthesis of rhodopsin and cone opsins in photoreceptors?

- **(A)** Vitamin A ✅
- **(B)** Vitamin B12
- **(C)** Vitamin C
- **(D)** Vitamin D
- **(E)** Vitamin K

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Vitamin A (retinol) is the precursor for 11-cis-retinal, the essential chromophore bound to opsins forming rhodopsin (in rods) and cone opsins (in cones). Vitamin A deficiency impairs rhodopsin synthesis, causing night blindness (nyctalopia) and xerophthalmia. Vitamin B12 (B) causes subacute combined degeneration; Vitamin C (C) causes scurvy; Vitamin D (D) causes rickets; Vitamin K (E) causes coagulopathy.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Vitamin A

## Question 58 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q37`

A 32-year-old woman is evaluated during a full-night polysomnogram. The sleep technologist notes that the patient experiences cyclical transitions between NREM and REM sleep throughout the night. Approximately how long is a normal, complete NREM-to-REM sleep cycle in a healthy adult?

- **(A)** 15 to 20 minutes
- **(B)** 30 to 45 minutes
- **(C)** 90 to 110 minutes ✅
- **(D)** 180 to 200 minutes
- **(E)** 300 minutes

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: Normal human sleep architecture cycles between NREM stages and REM sleep in regular intervals lasting approximately 90 to 110 minutes throughout the night. Options (A), (B), (D), and (E) do not represent the standard adult sleep cycle duration.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: 90 to 110 minutes

## Question 59 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q38`

A 62-year-old male presents with difficulty speaking and swallowing. Examination reveals tongue atrophy and prominent fasciculations across the tongue surface. When asked to extend his tongue, it deviates to the left. The patient also exhibits hyperactive deep tendon reflexes (3+) in all four extremities and an upgoing plantar reflex on the right foot. Sensory exam is completely normal. Which disease process involves simultaneous degeneration of both upper and lower motor neurons?

- **(A)** Decussation of the pyramids in the caudal medulla
- **(B)** Anterior white commissure in the cervical spinal cord ✅
- **(C)** Dorsal midbrain tegmentum at the interpeduncular fossa
- **(D)** Posterior limb of the internal capsule in the forebrain
- **(E)** Corona radiata within the subcortical cerebral white matter

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: ALS is characterized by simultaneous degeneration of UMNs (motor cortex Betz cells, lateral corticospinal tracts) and LMNs (cranial motor nuclei V, VII, IX, X, XII, and spinal anterior horns). Tongue atrophy and fasciculations with deviation (LMN) occurring alongside hyperreflexia, spasticity, and Babinski signs (UMN) without sensory loss is pathognomonic for ALS. Option (A) is a CNS demyelinating disease; Option (C) is posterior column syphilis; Option (D) is acute demyelinating polyneuropathy; Option (E) causes cape-like pain/temperature loss.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis
