## Question 60 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q31`

A 45-year-old male presents with an eyelid problem. Physical examination demonstrates mild drooping of the right upper eyelid (ptosis). The examiner determines that the ptosis is caused by loss of sympathetic tone to smooth muscle fibers within the upper eyelid. What is the name of this smooth muscle?

- **(A)** Levator palpebrae superioris muscle innervated by the oculomotor nerve
- **(B)** Superior tarsal muscle innervated by postganglionic sympathetic fibers ✅
- **(C)** Orbicularis oculi muscle innervated by the peripheral facial nerve
- **(D)** Superior rectus muscle innervated by the somatic oculomotor nerve
- **(E)** Inferior oblique muscle innervated by the somatic oculomotor nerve

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Eyelid elevation is mediated by two separate muscles: 1) the levator palpebrae superioris (a skeletal muscle innervated by CN III somatic motor fibers, providing primary lid elevation), and 2) the superior tarsal muscle (Müller's muscle, a smooth muscle innervated by postganglionic oculosympathetic fibers, providing resting lid tone). In Horner syndrome, loss of sympathetic innervation to the superior tarsal muscle results in mild to moderate upper eyelid drooping (partial ptosis). In contrast, CN III palsy causes complete, severe ptosis because the primary elevator (levator palpebrae superioris) is paralyzed.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Partial ptosis in Horner syndrome is caused by loss of sympathetic tone to the superior tarsal (Müller) muscle, whereas complete ptosis in CN III palsy involves levator palpebrae superioris.

---

## Question 61 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q4`

An emergency physician evaluates a comatose trauma patient. Shining a penlight into the patient's right eye fails to elicit pupillary constriction in either the right eye (absent direct response) or left eye (absent consensual response). However, when the penlight is shone into the left eye, both the left pupil (intact direct response) and right pupil (intact consensual response) constrict promptly. Where is the anatomical lesion located?

- **(A)** Left optic nerve anterior to the optic chiasm
- **(B)** Right optic nerve anterior to the optic chiasm ✅
- **(C)** Right oculomotor nerve exiting the midbrain
- **(D)** Left Edinger-Westphal nucleus in the rostral midbrain
- **(E)** Posterior commissure in the dorsal pretectal tegmentum

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The pupillary light reflex consists of an afferent sensory limb carried by the optic nerve (CN II) to the pretectal nuclei, and bilateral efferent parasympathetic limbs carried by the oculomotor nerves (CN III) from the Edinger-Westphal nuclei to the sphincter pupillae muscles via the ciliary ganglion. Shining light into the right eye produces no pupillary constriction in either eye (absent direct and consensual responses), indicating an afferent sensory defect in the right eye. Shining light into the left eye produces prompt, bilateral pupillary constriction (intact direct and consensual responses), confirming that both efferent CN III limbs and the left afferent CN II limb are intact. Therefore, the lesion localizes to the right optic nerve.

**High-Yield Pearl**: An isolated afferent pupillary defect produces bilateral absent pupillary responses when light is shone into the affected eye, but normal bilateral responses when light is shone into the intact eye.

---

## Question 62 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q5`

A 32-year-old construction worker suffers a traumatic spinal cord injury resulting in complete transection of the lateral spinal cord on the right side at the T8 vertebral level. Physical examination two weeks post-injury demonstrates motor deficits. Which of the following motor exam findings will be present below the level of the lesion on the right side?

- **(A)** Flaccid paralysis with rapid, severe muscle atrophy
- **(B)** Spastic hemiplegia with hyperreflexia and Babinski sign ✅
- **(C)** Fasciculations with absent deep tendon reflexes
- **(D)** Complete absence of voluntary movement with intact, normal plantar flexor response
- **(E)** Loss of deep tendon reflexes with normal muscle tone

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Hemisection of the spinal cord at T8 interrupts the descending lateral corticospinal tract on the ipsilateral side below the pyramidal decussation. Because fibers have already decussated in the medulla, interruption of the corticospinal tract in the thoracic cord produces ipsilateral upper motor neuron signs below the level of the lesion (spasticity, hyperactive reflexes, upgoing Babinski sign). Option (A) describes LMN damage at the exact segment level; Option (C) represents LMN degeneration; Option (D) incorrectly assumes a flexor plantar response; Option (E) describes LMN hyporeflexia.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Spastic hemiplegia with hyperreflexia and Babinski sign

---

## Question 63 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q25`

A 75-year-old female is evaluated for memory decline and poor sleep quality. Polysomnography demonstrates a dramatic age-related reduction in the total duration of slow-wave sleep (N3 stage). Which physiological process is primarily impaired when N3 slow-wave sleep is significantly reduced?

- **(A)** Execution of rapid eye movement ocular saccades
- **(B)** Deep tissue restoration and peak growth hormone release ✅
- **(C)** Consolidation of emotional nightmares
- **(D)** Generation of nocturnal penile/clitoral tumescence
- **(E)** Maintenance of skeletal muscle tone during dreaming

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: N3 slow-wave sleep is restorative: peak growth hormone release occurs during early N3 sleep to facilitate tissue repair and protein synthesis, and metabolic/sympathetic activity reaches its nadir. Aging decreases N3 duration. Option (A) is REM; Option (C) is REM; Option (D) is REM; Option (E) is REM.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Deep tissue restoration and peak growth hormone release

---

## Question 64 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q5`

A 52-year-old male smoker presents with right-sided drooping of the upper eyelid (ptosis), a constricted right pupil (miosis), and complete absence of sweating over the right side of his face (anhidrosis). Chest radiography reveals a mass in the apex of the right lung (Pancoast tumor). Compression of which autonomic structure accounts for this patient's Horner syndrome?

- **(A)** Edinger-Westphal nucleus in the rostral midbrain tegmentum
- **(B)** Sympathetic trunk and superior cervical ganglion at the lung apex ✅
- **(C)** Ciliary ganglion located within the posterior orbital cavity
- **(D)** Cavernous sinus parasympathetic plexus surrounding CN III
- **(E)** Paramedian pontine reticular formation in the caudal pons

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: A superior pulmonary sulcus tumor (Pancoast tumor) originates in the apex of the lung and frequently invades contiguous neurovascular structures at the thoracic outlet, including the cervical sympathetic chain and the stellate / inferior cervical ganglion. Disruption of preganglionic sympathetic fibers ascending from the T1 intermediolateral cell column produces Horner syndrome, characterized by the classic triad of partial ptosis (loss of superior tarsal muscle tone), miosis (unopposed parasympathetic sphincter pupillae activity), and facial anhidrosis (loss of sympathetic innervation to eccrine sweat glands).

**High-Yield Pearl**: Pancoast tumors at the lung apex compress the sympathetic trunk and stellate ganglion, causing ipsilateral Horner syndrome (ptosis, miosis, anhidrosis).

---

## Question 65 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q45`

A 22-year-old patient presents with diplopia. Examination shows that when looking to the right, the right eye abducts normally, but the left eye fails to adduct. Convergence during accommodation is completely normal. What is the diagnosis?

- **(A)** Left internuclear ophthalmoplegia from a demyelinating MLF lesion ✅
- **(B)** Left oculomotor nerve palsy from compressive posterior vascular aneurysm
- **(C)** Left abducens nerve palsy from microvascular ischemic pontine stroke
- **(D)** Right frontal eye field stroke from middle cerebral artery infarction
- **(E)** Parinaud dorsal midbrain syndrome from pineal gland compression

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: On attempted rightward horizontal gaze, the left eye fails to adduct past the midline, while the right eye abducts normally with horizontal nystagmus. Normal convergence during accommodation proves that the left medial rectus muscle and left oculomotor nucleus are completely functional. This selective adduction deficit on conjugate horizontal gaze with preserved convergence localizes the lesion to the LEFT medial longitudinal fasciculus (MLF), defining a left internuclear ophthalmoplegia (INO). In a 22-year-old patient, multiple sclerosis is the most common etiology.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Adduction failure on contralateral horizontal gaze with abducting nystagmus and preserved convergence defines internuclear ophthalmoplegia (INO) on the side of the adduction deficit.

---

## Question 66 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q55`

An anatomical study reviews the cavernous sinus. Which of the following groups of cranial nerves and associated vascular structures passes through or within the cavernous sinus?

- **(A)** CN III, CN IV, CN V1, CN V2, and CN VI along with the internal carotid artery ✅
- **(B)** CN III, CN IV, CN V3, and CN VII along with the middle meningeal artery
- **(C)** CN II, CN III, CN IV, and CN VI along with the ophthalmic artery
- **(D)** CN IX, CN X, CN XI, and CN XII along with the internal jugular vein
- **(E)** CN V1, CN V2, CN V3, and CN VII along with the facial venous plexus

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The cavernous sinus is a trabeculated dural venous sinus situated on either side of the sella turcica. Traversing its lateral dural wall from superior to inferior are the oculomotor nerve (CN III), trochlear nerve (CN IV), ophthalmic nerve (CN V1), and maxillary nerve (CN V2). Running freely through the central venous lumen, surrounded by venous blood and situated immediately adjacent to the internal carotid artery, is the abducens nerve (CN VI). The mandibular nerve (CN V3) does NOT pass through the cavernous sinus; it exits the middle cranial fossa directly via the foramen ovale.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Structures in the cavernous sinus: CN III, IV, V1, V2 in the lateral wall; CN VI and internal carotid artery in the central venous lumen. CN V3 does NOT pass through.

---

## Question 67 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q51`

An investigator measures action potential generation in retinal cells. Which retinal cell layer contains the first order neurons that fire action potentials along their central processes to form the optic nerve?

- **(A)** Photoreceptor cells
- **(B)** Bipolar cells
- **(C)** Retinal ganglion cells ✅
- **(D)** Horizontal cells
- **(E)** Amacrine cells

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: Retinal ganglion cells are 3rd order sensory neurons in the visual pathway. Unlike photoreceptors (1st order) and bipolar cells (2nd order)—which communicate via graded electrotonic potentials—retinal ganglion cells generate true action potentials that travel along their central axons forming the optic nerve, chiasm, and tract to the LGN. Options (A), (B), (D), and (E) do not form the optic nerve.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Retinal ganglion cells

---

## Question 68 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q49`

A 36-year-old female presents with brief, recurrent episodes of vertigo when turning her head to the left while lying in bed. A Dix-Hallpike test is performed, producing transient horizontal-rotatory nystagmus. The patient is successfully treated with an Epley maneuver. What is the underlying physical mechanism of the Epley maneuver?

- **(A)** Dissolving calcium carbonate crystals using topical acid drops
- **(B)** Mechanically repositioning dislodged otoconia out of the semicircular canal into the utricle ✅
- **(C)** Ablating the vestibular ganglion using focused gamma radiation
- **(D)** Depleting endolymphatic fluid pressure via a surgical shunt
- **(E)** Suppressing neurotransmitter release in the cochlear nucleus

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The Epley maneuver uses gravity through sequential head rotations to roll free-floating otoconia out of the posterior semicircular canal back into the utricle, relieving BPPV. Option (A) is incorrect; Option (C) is for tumors; Option (D) is for Ménière disease; Option (E) is pharmacological.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Mechanically repositioning dislodged otoconia out of the semicircular canal into the utricle

## Question 69 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q52`

A 46-year-old male presents with progressive weakness in both hands over the past 8 months. Examination shows severe atrophy of the interosseous muscles bilaterally, with fasciculations visible in the arms and chest. Deep tendon reflexes are 3+ in the biceps, triceps, patellar, and Achilles tendons bilaterally. Plantar responses are extensor bilaterally. Sensory perception is intact throughout. What is the fundamental cellular pathology?

- **(A)** Autoimmune demyelination of central white matter pathways
- **(B)** Simultaneous degeneration of cortical upper motor neurons and spinal lower motor neurons ✅
- **(C)** Selective destruction of dorsal root ganglion cell bodies
- **(D)** Ischemic infarction of the anterior spinal artery territory
- **(E)** Compression of the cauda equina nerve roots

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Simultaneous presence of UMN signs (generalized hyperreflexia, ankle clonus, extensor plantar responses) and LMN signs (hand interosseous muscle atrophy, fasciculations, weakness) without sensory deficits is pathognomonic for Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis (ALS). The underlying cellular pathology is progressive degeneration of both cortical UMNs (Betz cells/corticospinal tracts) and spinal/bulbar LMNs (alpha motor neurons in anterior horns and brainstem motor nuclei). Option (A) describes multiple sclerosis; Option (C) causes sensory loss; Option (D) causes acute anterior cord infarction; Option (E) causes lumbosacral LMN root compression.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Simultaneous degeneration of cortical upper motor neurons and spinal lower motor neurons

## Question 70 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q32`

A 58-year-old woman is brought to the clinic after tripping over objects located on her right side. Perimetry confirms a right homonymous hemianopia without macular sparing. A complete lesion of which of the following structures in the left hemisphere will cause this presentation?

- **(A)** Left optic tract ✅
- **(B)** Right optic nerve
- **(C)** Optic chiasm
- **(D)** Left Meyer loop exclusively
- **(E)** Right lingual gyrus

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Complete right homonymous hemianopia without macular sparing results from a total lesion of the left optic tract, left LGN, or left optic radiations. Right optic nerve (B) causes right monocular blindness; Optic chiasm (C) causes bitemporal hemianopia; Left Meyer loop (D) causes right upper quadrantanopia; Right lingual gyrus (E) causes left upper quadrantanopia.

## Question 71 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q51`

An animal experiment investigates the vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR). When the head is rotated to the left at a constant angular velocity, primary vestibular afferents from the left horizontal semicircular canal increase their firing rate. These signals project to the left vestibular nucleus, which sends decussating excitatory axons to the contralateral right abducens nucleus (CN VI). What eye movements are produced to maintain visual fixation?

- **(A)** Both eyes deviate to the LEFT
- **(B)** Both eyes deviate to the RIGHT ✅
- **(C)** Both eyes elevate UPWARD
- **(D)** Both eyes depress DOWNWARD
- **(E)** Both eyes converge MEDIALLY

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The VOR drives compensatory eye movements opposite to head rotation. Turning the head to the left stimulates the left vestibular nucleus, which projects across the midline to the right abducens nucleus (CN VI). The right abducens contracts the right lateral rectus and projects via the MLF to contract the left medial rectus, smoothly deviating both eyes to the RIGHT. Options (A), (C), (D), and (E) misidentify VOR trajectory.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Both eyes deviate to the RIGHT

---

## Question 72 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q46`

A 19-year-old college student presents with severe right ear pain, itching, and discharge after spending the weekend on a swim team trip. Examination demonstrates severe pain when the clinician gently pulls the right pinna upward and backward. The tympanic membrane is intact and mobile. What is the diagnosis?

- **(A)** Acute otitis media with purulent middle ear effusion
- **(B)** Acute otitis externa affecting the external acoustic canal ✅
- **(C)** Otosclerosis with progressive stapes ankylosis
- **(D)** Cholesteatoma invading the mastoid antrum cavity
- **(E)** Tympanosclerosis involving the tympanic membrane

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Otitis externa ("Swimmer's ear") is an acute external canal infection causing severe pain elicited by pulling the pinna or tragus, with canal edema and exudate, while the eardrum remains intact and mobile. Option (A) causes bulging eardrum and fever; Option (C) causes vertigo and hearing loss; Option (D) causes post-auricular tenderness over mastoid; Option (E) is a chronic middle ear mass.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Otitis externa (Swimmer's ear)

---

## Question 73 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q28`

A 40-year-old female presents with progressive leg weakness and spasticity. Neurological examination reveals lower extremity hyperreflexia, bilateral ankle clonus, and upgoing toes on plantar stimulation. Upper extremity motor exam is normal. A lesion affecting which of the following descending motor pathways is responsible for her elevated muscle tone and clonus?

- **(A)** Lateral corticospinal and cortico-bulbo-spinal pathways ✅
- **(B)** Ventral primary rami of the lumbar plexus
- **(C)** Primary afferent fibers from muscle spindles
- **(D)** Alpha motor neurons in the anterior horn
- **(E)** Postganglionic sympathetic fibers

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Descending upper motor neuron pathways (corticospinal and cortico-bulbo-spinal, including reticulospinal/rubrospinal tracts) continuously suppress alpha motor neuron excitability and damp down monosynaptic stretch reflexes. Interruption of these UMN pathways removes descending tonic inhibition, causing hyperreflexia, clonus, spasticity, and Babinski sign. Option (B) causes LMN flaccid paralysis; Option (C) abolishes stretch reflexes; Option (D) causes LMN flaccidity and atrophy; Option (E) regulates autonomic vascular/sweat functions.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Lateral corticospinal and cortico-bulbo-spinal pathways

---

## Question 74 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q28`

A 65-year-old male suffers an ischemic stroke in the territory of the left middle cerebral artery (MCA) deep penetrating branches, damaging the left optic radiations in the internal capsule. What visual field deficit will be produced?

- **(A)** Right homonymous hemianopia ✅
- **(B)** Bitemporal hemianopia
- **(C)** Left homonymous hemianopia
- **(D)** Monocular blindness in right eye
- **(E)** Binasal hemianopia

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Complete interruption of optic radiations in the left hemisphere (e.g., internal capsule stroke) disconnects all visual input from the right visual hemifield (right upper and lower fields from both eyes), producing a complete right homonymous hemianopia without macular sparing. Chiasm (B) causes bitemporal loss; Right radiations (C) cause left homonymous loss; Right optic nerve (D) causes right eye blindness; Binasal (E) requires bilateral lateral chiasm compression.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right homonymous hemianopia

---

## Question 75 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q21`

A 40-year-old female presents for a routine eye exam. The clinician performs fundoscopy to inspect the fovea. What unique structural and functional characteristic defines the fovea centralis?

- **(A)** It contains high density of rods and mediates dim light peripheral vision
- **(B)** It is the site where optic nerve axons exit the retina, creating a blind spot
- **(C)** It is the point of highest visual acuity, containing exclusively cones ✅
- **(D)** It secretes aqueous humor into the posterior chamber
- **(E)** It is perfused directly by the posterior spinal artery

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: The fovea centralis (located in the center of the macula) is specialized for maximum visual acuity and color vision. It contains exclusively cone photoreceptors with 1:1 synaptic connections to bipolar and ganglion cells and displaced overlying neural layers. Rods (A) predominate in peripheral retina; Optic disc (B) lacks photoreceptors; Ciliary body (D) secretes fluid; Posterior spinal artery (E) supplies spinal cord.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: It is the point of highest visual acuity, containing exclusively cones

---

## Question 76 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q18`

A patient is evaluated following a traumatic brain injury. The examiner notes that visual signals originating from the lower retina travel via Meyer's loop through the temporal lobe. Which portion of the visual field is carried by fibers in Meyer's loop?

- **(A)** Lower visual field
- **(B)** Upper visual field ✅
- **(C)** Nasal visual field exclusively
- **(D)** Central macula exclusively
- **(E)** Ipsilateral visual hemifield

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Fibers in Meyer's loop loop anteriorly through the temporal lobe, carrying signals from the lower retina. Because optics invert images, the lower retina detects stimuli from the upper visual field. Therefore, Meyer's loop carries upper visual field information. Options (A), (C), (D), and (E) misattribute spatial orientation.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Upper visual field

---

## Question 77 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q22`

A 22-year-old male is brought to the emergency department following a knife fight. He sustained a stab wound to the back at the level of the T4 spinal segment. Neurological exam demonstrates complete loss of voluntary motor control and loss of vibration/position sense in the right leg. Conversely, he has complete loss of pain and temperature sensation in the left leg starting at the T6 dermatome level. What is the clinical diagnosis for this hemisection of the spinal cord?

- **(A)** Anterior cord syndrome
- **(B)** Central cord syndrome
- **(C)** Brown-Séquard syndrome ✅
- **(D)** Cauda equina syndrome
- **(E)** Tabes dorsalis

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: Functional hemisection of the spinal cord (Brown-Séquard syndrome) at T4 interrupts three major tracts: ipsilateral lateral corticospinal tract (ipsilateral UMN leg weakness below lesion), ipsilateral dorsal column (ipsilateral loss of fine touch/proprioception below lesion), and decussating anterolateral system (contralateral loss of pain and temperature starting 1–2 segments below lesion, i.e., T6). Option (A) causes bilateral paralysis and pain/temperature loss; Option (B) affects upper limbs more than lower; Option (D) causes lumbosacral root compression; Option (E) causes posterior column degeneration.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Brown-Séquard syndrome

---

## Question 78 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q16`

A patient diagnosed with Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV) involving the posterior semicircular canal undergoes a therapeutic repositioning procedure in the clinic. The clinician rotates the patient's head through a precise sequence of supine positions to guide displaced otoconia back into the vestibule. What is the name of this classic clinical maneuver?

- **(A)** Dix-Hallpike maneuver
- **(B)** Epley maneuver ✅
- **(C)** Romberg maneuver
- **(D)** Valsalva maneuver
- **(E)** Head-thrust maneuver

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The Epley maneuver (canalith repositioning procedure) uses sequential head rotations to guide free-floating otoconia out of the posterior semicircular canal back into the utricle. Dix-Hallpike is the diagnostic test. Option (A) is diagnostic; Option (C) tests proprioception/balance; Option (D) increases intrathoracic pressure; Option (E) evaluates VOR.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Epley maneuver

---

## Question 79 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q6`

A 45-year-old factory worker presents with progressive, bilateral high-frequency hearing loss. He reports working in a high-noise industrial stamping facility for 20 years without wearing hearing protection. An audiogram confirms sensorineural hearing loss, with maximal threshold elevation at 4000 Hz. Which cellular structures within the organ of Corti are most vulnerable to irreversible damage from chronic, high-intensity noise exposure?

- **(A)** Primary auditory neurons in the medial geniculate nucleus
- **(B)** Outer hair cells at the basal turn of the cochlea ✅
- **(C)** Inner hair cells at the apical turn of the cochlea
- **(D)** Gelatinous otolithic membrane of the utricle
- **(E)** Fibrous connective tissue of the tympanic membrane

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Chronic high-intensity noise trauma selectively damages outer hair cells in the organ of Corti. High-frequency sound waves enter via the oval window and cause maximum basilar membrane displacement near the base of the cochlea, making basal hair cells most vulnerable and producing classic 4000 Hz notch sensorineural hearing loss. Option (A) is central; Option (C) apical hair cells transduce low frequencies; Option (D) is vestibular; Option (E) is eardrum tissue.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Outer hair cells at the basal turn of the cochlea

## Question 80 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q11`

A 21-year-old female with narcolepsy experiences frequent disruptive episodes of cataplexy triggered by emotional events like laughing or being startled. In addition to scheduled daytime naps and modafinil for daytime wakefulness, which of the following therapeutic choices is effective for suppressing cataplexy?

- **(A)** Zolpidem
- **(B)** Sodium oxybate ✅
- **(C)** Ramelteon
- **(D)** Diphenhydramine
- **(E)** Triazolam

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Cataplexy in narcolepsy is managed with sodium oxybate (or REM-suppressing antidepressants like SSRIs/SNRIs), which improves nocturnal sleep architecture and suppresses daytime cataplexy. Modafinil treats daytime sleepiness. Option (A) is for insomnia; Option (C) is a melatonin agonist for insomnia; Option (D) is an antihistamine that increases sleepiness; Option (E) is a benzodiazepine.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Sodium oxybate

## Question 81 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q12`

A 47-year-old male presents with severe left periorbital pain, fever, swelling around his left eye, and double vision following a dental abscess. Physical examination reveals total ophthalmoplegia of the left eye (inability to move left eye in any direction), drooping of the left upper eyelid, a dilated left pupil, and complete anesthesia over the left forehead and cheek (CN V1/V2 distributions). MRI demonstrates a severe infection spreading via ophthalmic veins. What structure is affected?

- **(A)** Jugular foramen in the posterior cranial fossa
- **(B)** Cavernous sinus located lateral to the sella turcica ✅
- **(C)** Foramen rotundum in the greater wing of the sphenoid
- **(D)** Internal acoustic meatus in the petrous temporal bone
- **(E)** Foramen magnum at the base of the occipital bone

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The clinical presentation of severe periorbital pain, chemosis, proptosis, fever, total ophthalmoplegia (inability to move the eye due to paresis of CN III, IV, and VI), and sensory loss over the forehead and cheek (CN V1 and V2) following a dental or facial infection is the classic presentation of cavernous sinus thrombosis (CST). The cavernous sinus is a trabeculated venous structure situated bilaterally on either side of the sella turcica. Because facial and ophthalmic veins are valveless, infections from the "danger triangle" of the face or dental abscesses can spread retrograde into the cavernous sinus, compressing the cranial nerves that traverse its lateral wall (CN III, IV, V1, V2) and central lumen (CN VI).

**High-Yield Pearl**: Cavernous sinus thrombosis presents with ophthalmoplegia (CN III, IV, VI), V1/V2 sensory loss, and proptosis following facial or orbital infection via valveless veins.

---

## Question 82 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q26`

An investigator tests phototransduction mechanics. In total darkness, photoreceptors maintain a steady resting membrane potential and continuously release glutamate. What bioelectric change occurs in a photoreceptor when light absorbs into rhodopsin or cone opsins?

- **(A)** Massive influx of sodium leading to depolarization
- **(B)** Hyperpolarization of the cell membrane and decreased glutamate release ✅
- **(C)** Sudden action potential firing along the photoreceptor axon
- **(D)** Release of acetylcholine into the vitreous humor
- **(E)** Efflux of calcium leading to rapid cellular destruction

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Photoreceptors are unique sensory cells: in darkness, they are depolarized and continuously release glutamate. Light absorption isomerizes 11-cis-retinal to all-trans-retinal, activating cGMP phosphodiesterase (PDE), closing Na+ channels, hyperpolarizing the photoreceptor, and decreasing glutamate release onto bipolar cells. Options (A), (C), (D), and (E) misrepresent phototransduction bioelectricity.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Hyperpolarization of the cell membrane and decreased glutamate release

---

## Question 83 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q30`

An anatomical cross-section of the rostral midbrain demonstrates the oculomotor nucleus and Edinger-Westphal nucleus. Fibers crossing in which commissure connect the pretectal nucleus bilaterally to both Edinger-Westphal nuclei?

- **(A)** Anterior commissure crossing between the anterior temporal lobes
- **(B)** Posterior commissure crossing through the dorsal pretectal midbrain ✅
- **(C)** Corpus callosum rostrum carrying interhemispheric frontal connections
- **(D)** Habenular commissure carrying interconnections between epithalamic nuclei
- **(E)** Optic chiasm decussating nasal retinal fibers from both ocular retinas

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The anatomical cross-coupling that underlies the consensual pupillary light reflex relies on decussating fibers in the dorsal rostral midbrain. When retinal ganglion cells stimulate pretectal neurons on one side, each pretectal nucleus projects bilaterally: ipsilaterally to the uncrossed Edinger-Westphal nucleus, and contralaterally to the opposite Edinger-Westphal nucleus. The crossing interneuronal axons pass through the posterior commissure. Interruption of the posterior commissure eliminates the consensual light reflex across the eyes while preserving unilateral direct pathways.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Consensual pupillary light reflex fibers crossing from the pretectal nucleus to the contralateral Edinger-Westphal nucleus travel through the posterior commissure.

---

## Question 84 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q44`

A 40-year-old female presents with visual distortion. She can perceive light and shapes normally, but cannot recognize familiar faces (prosopagnosia) despite normal visual sensation. Visual agnosia results from damage to which cortical region?

- **(A)** Primary visual cortex striate area V1
- **(B)** Visual association cortex areas V2 and V3 ✅
- **(C)** Precentral gyrus primary motor cortex
- **(D)** Postcentral gyrus primary somatosensory cortex
- **(E)** Superior temporal gyrus auditory cortex

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Visual agnosia is a cognitive perceptual deficit where primary visual sensation (V1) is intact, but visual association cortex (neighboring occipitotemporal/parietal pathways) is damaged, preventing higher-order interpretation and identification of visual objects or faces (prosopagnosia). V1 (A) damage causes cortical blindness/hemianopia; Precentral (C) is motor; Postcentral (D) is somatosensory; Temporal (E) is auditory.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Visual association cortex

---

## Question 85 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q31`

A neuro-otologist performs caloric reflex testing on an awake patient with normal brainstem and cortical function. Cold water is infused into the patient's right external auditory canal. Which pattern of ocular nystagmus (named by the fast resetting phase) will be produced in this healthy individual?

- **(A)** Fast phase beating to the RIGHT toward the irrigated ear
- **(B)** Fast phase beating to the LEFT away from the irrigated ear ✅
- **(C)** Pure vertical upward nystagmus without horizontal motion
- **(D)** Pure vertical downward nystagmus without horizontal motion
- **(E)** Total cessation of all spontaneous and evoked ocular motion

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Caloric testing follows the COWS mnemonic (Cold = Opposite, Warm = Same) for the fast resetting phase of nystagmus. Cold water in the right ear chills horizontal canal endolymph, decreasing baseline right vestibular firing. Eyes slowly drift toward the right ear (slow phase), followed by a rapid cortical fast phase beating to the LEFT (Opposite side). Option (A) occurs with warm water; Option (C) indicates central pathology; Option (D) indicates brainstem death; Option (E) is incorrect.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Fast phase beating to the LEFT (away from the irrigated ear)

---

## Question 86 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q11`

A 67-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after his family noticed sudden weakness on the right side of his face. On examination, he has weakness of his right upper facial muscles and lower facial muscles, with an inability to smile, raise his right eyebrow, or close his right eye tightly. Body strength is entirely normal bilaterally. Which of the following conditions best matches these clinical findings?

- **(A)** Right-sided supranuclear corticobulbar lesion sparing upper facial muscles
- **(B)** Right-sided peripheral facial nerve palsy affecting all ipsilateral muscles ✅
- **(C)** Left-sided primary motor cortex infarction within the precentral gyrus
- **(D)** Left-sided internal capsule posterior limb lacunar microvascular infarction
- **(E)** Bilateral pontine tegmental demyelination of corticobulbar projection tracts

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: A lower motor neuron lesion of CN VII (Bell palsy) injures the nerve distal to the motor nucleus, producing paralysis of all ipsilateral facial expression muscles, affecting both the upper face (forehead, eye closure) and lower face (smile, nasolabial fold). Option (A) describes an MCA stroke which causes contralateral lower face droop with upper forehead sparing (UMN pattern); Option (C) causes lower facial droop with forehead sparing; Option (D) spares the face; Option (E) causes pharyngeal/laryngeal paralysis.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right-sided Bell palsy (infranuclear CN VII lesion)

---

## Question 87 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q18`

A 33-year-old male presents to the urgent care clinic complaining of sudden, continuous room-spinning vertigo, severe nausea, and instability that began 24 hours ago following a viral upper respiratory infection. He also reports new hearing loss and ringing in his left ear. Neurological examination reveals horizontal nystagmus beating toward the right. MRI of the brain is completely unremarkable. What is the most likely diagnosis?

- **(A)** Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo
- **(B)** Acute labyrinthitis ✅
- **(C)** Vestibular schwannoma
- **(D)** Vertebrobasilar insufficiency
- **(E)** Ménière disease

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Acute labyrinthitis involves inflammation of both vestibular and cochlear divisions of CN VIII (or inner ear labyrinth), often following a viral URI. It presents with continuous severe vertigo, nausea, and gait instability lasting days, combined with sensorineural hearing loss and tinnitus. Vestibular neuritis spares hearing. Option (A) causes brief positional vertigo (<1 min) without hearing loss; Option (C) causes slow progressive loss; Option (D) causes central TIAs; Option (E) causes episodic attacks lasting hours.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Acute labyrinthitis

---

## Question 88 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q35`

A 59-year-old woman presents with persistent balance loss and difficulty walking straight. Physical examination shows a positive Romberg sign with eyes closed. When her head is rapidly turned to the right while focusing on the examiner's nose, her eyes slip off the target and make a compensatory catch-up saccade back to the nose (positive head-thrust test on the right). What structure is damaged?

- **(A)** Cerebellar hemisphere or vermal flocculonodular lesion
- **(B)** Peripheral vestibular labyrinth or vestibular nerve lesion ✅
- **(C)** Medial longitudinal fasciculus brainstem tegmental lesion
- **(D)** Frontal eye field cortical precentral motor stroke
- **(E)** Paramedian pontine reticular formation pontine stroke

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The head-thrust test evaluates VOR integrity. When the head is rapidly turned to the side of a peripheral vestibular lesion (right side), the impaired VOR fails to maintain fixation on the target. The eyes drift off target and make a compensatory catch-up saccade back to the target, confirming a right peripheral vestibular or CN VIII lesion. Options (A), (C), (D), and (E) do not produce a positive head-thrust test to the right.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right peripheral vestibular apparatus or CN VIII vestibular division

---

## Question 89 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q8`

A 58-year-old male presents with difficulty swallowing and slurred speech that has gradually worsened over four months. Inspection of the oral cavity reveals a normal, non-atrophic tongue with no fasciculations. When asked to protrude his tongue, it remains strictly in the midline. Gag reflex is brisk. Muscle tone in his limbs is elevated with hyperactive deep tendon reflexes. Which of the following best explains the presence of pseudobulbar motor features in this patient?

- **(A)** Bilateral lower motor neuron lesion of the trigeminal motor nucleus
- **(B)** Bilateral upper motor neuron lesion of the corticobulbar tract above the brainstem motor nuclei ✅
- **(C)** Unilateral peripheral lesion of the mandibular branch of CN V
- **(D)** Isolated LMN degeneration within the nucleus ambiguus
- **(E)** Compressive lesion of the hypoglossal nerve in the hypoglossal canal

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Cranial nerve motor nuclei (except lower face and tongue) receive bilateral corticobulbar UMN projections. Bilateral corticobulbar tract lesions above the brainstem interrupt supranuclear inhibitory input, producing pseudobulbar motor features (hyperactive reflexes, dysarthria, spastic bulbar weakness) while sparing lower motor neurons, thereby preventing muscle atrophy and fasciculations. Option (A) causes LMN jaw muscle atrophy and absent reflexes; Option (C) causes ipsilateral jaw weakness; Option (D) causes LMN dysphagia; Option (E) causes LMN tongue atrophy.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Bilateral upper motor neuron lesion of the corticobulbar tract above the brainstem motor nuclei

---

## Question 90 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q31`

A 66-year-old male presents with an acute stroke involving the left crus cerebri of the midbrain. Which combination of clinical motor deficits will be observed on physical examination?

- **(A)** Right-sided spastic body hemiplegia and right lower face droop ✅
- **(B)** Left-sided flaccid body hemiplegia and left total face paralysis
- **(C)** Right-sided body flaccidity with absent deep tendon reflexes
- **(D)** Ipsilateral loss of fine touch and pain sensation across the body
- **(E)** Bilateral complete body paralysis with preserved facial movements

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The crus cerebri in the ventral midbrain contains uncrossed descending corticospinal and corticobulbar UMN fibers. A lesion of the left crus cerebri interrupts motor drive prior to decussation, producing contralateral (right-sided) spastic body hemiplegia (corticospinal tract) and contralateral (right-sided) lower facial droop (corticobulbar tract) with forehead sparing. Option (B) mislocates side and describes LMN flaccidity; Option (C) describes LMN deficits; Option (D) describes sensory loss; Option (E) describes bilateral quadriplegia.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right-sided spastic body hemiplegia and right lower face droop

---

## Question 91 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q53`

A 60-year-old male presents with a stroke in the caudal pons on the right side. Examination shows paralysis of rightward horizontal gaze in both eyes, total right-sided lower motor neuron facial paralysis, and right eye abduction failure. Which nuclear complex in the caudal pons is damaged?

- **(A)** Right facial colliculus involving the abducens nucleus and internal genu of CN VII ✅
- **(B)** Right oculomotor nucleus and adjacent Edinger-Westphal parasympathetic nucleus
- **(C)** Left frontal eye field within the posterior precentral cerebral cortex
- **(D)** Left paramedian pontine reticular formation in the caudal pontine tegmentum
- **(E)** Right trochlear nucleus located in the caudal midbrain periaqueductal gray

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The facial colliculus is a visible elevation on the dorsal floor of the fourth ventricle in the caudal pons. It is formed by motor axons of the facial nerve (CN VII) as they loop around the abducens nucleus (CN VI) before exiting the ventrolateral brainstem. A lesion in this exact region simultaneously destroys the abducens nucleus (causing complete horizontal conjugate gaze palsy to the ipsilateral right side) and the surrounding internal genu of the facial nerve (causing ipsilateral lower motor neuron facial paralysis involving both the upper and lower face).

**High-Yield Pearl**: A facial colliculus lesion in the dorsal caudal pons causes an ipsilateral horizontal gaze palsy (CN VI nucleus) and ipsilateral LMN facial paralysis (CN VII loop).

---

## Question 92 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q47`

A 67-year-old male presents with dysarthria. Examination demonstrates that when he protrudes his tongue, it deviates to the left side. Inspection of the tongue reveals pronounced atrophy and active fasciculations on the left side. Examination of the body shows 5/5 strength throughout, with 2+ deep tendon reflexes and downgoing toes bilaterally. What type of lesion is present?

- **(A)** Right UMN lesion of the corticobulbar tract
- **(B)** Left LMN lesion of the hypoglossal nerve/nucleus ✅
- **(C)** Left UMN lesion of the corticobulbar tract
- **(D)** Right LMN lesion of the hypoglossal nerve/nucleus
- **(E)** Bilateral UMN lesion of the motor cortex

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Lower motor neurons in the hypoglossal nucleus project via CN XII to the ipsilateral genioglossus muscle. An LMN lesion of the left hypoglossal nerve/nucleus produces left tongue atrophy, active fasciculations, and tongue deviation toward the weak/lesioned side (left) upon protrusion. Option (A) causes contralateral tongue deviation without atrophy/fasciculations; Option (C) causes right tongue deviation without atrophy; Option (D) causes right tongue atrophy and right deviation; Option (E) causes pseudobulbar speech without isolated tongue atrophy.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Left LMN lesion of the hypoglossal nerve/nucleus

## Question 93 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q9`

A 62-year-old female presents with a gradual, painless decline in vision in her right eye over the past year. Examination demonstrates central visual field loss in her right eye, while vision in her left eye is completely normal. What is the location of the lesion causing this monocular visual deficit?

- **(A)** Optic chiasm compression by a pituitary tumor
- **(B)** Left optic tract carrying contralateral hemifields
- **(C)** Right optic nerve anterior to the optic chiasm ✅
- **(D)** Left temporal lobe Meyer loop fibers in white matter
- **(E)** Right primary visual cortex in the occipital pole

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: Monocular visual field loss (loss of vision restricted to one eye) indicates a lesion located anterior to the optic chiasm, involving either the ipsilateral optic nerve or retina. Lesions at or posterior to the optic chiasm affect visual fields in both eyes. Options (A), (B), (D), and (E) cause binocular visual field defects.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Anterior to the optic chiasm (right optic nerve or right retina)

## Question 94 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q23`

A 25-year-old patient reports that when awakening in the morning, he occasionally experiences a terrifying sensation where he is fully conscious and aware of his surroundings, but completely unable to move his limbs, trunk, or speak for 1 to 2 minutes. What is the physiological mechanism underlying this phenomenon (sleep paralysis)?

- **(A)** Sudden onset of generalized epileptic seizure activity
- **(B)** Persistence of REM sleep muscle atonia into conscious wakefulness ✅
- **(C)** Ischemic infarction of the bilateral pyramids in the medulla
- **(D)** Paroxysmal hyperpolarization of dorsal column sensory neurons
- **(E)** Acute autoantibody blockade of skeletal muscle nicotinic receptors

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Sleep paralysis is a benign intrusion of REM muscle atonia into wakefulness. Brainstem projections hyperpolarize spinal motor neurons during REM sleep to prevent dream enactment. Sleep paralysis occurs when conscious awareness awakens while REM somatic muscle atonia persists for 1–2 minutes. Options (A), (C), (D), and (E) do not explain transient post-dormital paralysis.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Persistence of REM sleep muscle atonia into conscious wakefulness

---

## Question 95 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q20`

A 28-year-old male presents with astigmatism. Refractive evaluation demonstrates that images are unfocused on the retina due to an uneven shape of the ocular media. What structural irregularity causes astigmatism?

- **(A)** Eye axial length being too short
- **(B)** Irregular curvature of the cornea ✅
- **(C)** Stiffening of the lens with age
- **(D)** High intraocular pressure in the anterior chamber
- **(E)** Detachment of the retinal pigment epithelium

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Astigmatism results from asymmetrical, irregular curvature of the refractive surface of the cornea (or lens), preventing light rays from focusing onto a single focal point on the retina, causing blurred images. Eye too short (A) causes hyperopia; Lens stiffening (C) causes presbyopia; High IOP (D) causes glaucoma; RPE detachment (E) causes retinal detachment.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Irregular curvature of the cornea

---

## Question 96 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q19`

An investigator maps the pathway of visual signals from the retina to the cortex. Retinal ganglion cell axons carrying information from the nasal hemiretina cross the midline at the optic chiasm. Which portion of the visual field is detected by the nasal hemiretina of each eye?

- **(A)** Temporal visual field ✅
- **(B)** Nasal visual field
- **(C)** Upper central field exclusively
- **(D)** Lower central field exclusively
- **(E)** Ipsilateral visual field

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Lenses invert light rays laterally and vertically. Stimuli from the temporal (lateral) visual field strike the nasal (medial) hemiretina of that eye. Nasal retinal fibers decussate at the optic chiasm. Nasal visual field (B) strikes the temporal hemiretina.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Temporal visual field

---

## Question 97 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q22`

An experimental animal model evaluates posture and muscle tone following localized brainstem lesions. A transection interrupting the lateral vestibulospinal tract (LVST) is performed. Which of the following physiological deficits will be observed in the experimental subject?

- **(A)** Loss of neck muscle coordination for maintaining upright head position
- **(B)** Impairment of horizontal conjugate eye movements during head rotation
- **(C)** Loss of uncrossed tone in antigravity extensor muscles, causing instability ✅
- **(D)** Total loss of high-frequency hearing in the contralateral ear
- **(E)** Complete paralysis of facial expression muscles

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: The lateral vestibulospinal tract (LVST) originates in the lateral vestibular nucleus (Deiters nucleus) and descends uncrossed (ipsilaterally) down the spinal cord to excite alpha/gamma motor neurons innervating antigravity extensor muscles. Disruption causes loss of ipsilateral extensor tone and balance instability. Option (A) is MVST function; Option (B) is VOR/MLF function; Option (D) is lateral lemniscus; Option (E) is facial nerve function.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Loss of uncrossed tone in antigravity extensor muscles, causing instability

---

## Question 98 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q14`

A 48-year-old male with persistent insomnia is prescribed suvorexant. What is the precise mechanism of action of suvorexant in treating insomnia?

- **(A)** Agonist at melatonin MT1 and MT2 G-protein coupled receptors
- **(B)** Selective allosteric modulator at the GABA-A receptor complex
- **(C)** Competitive antagonist at dual OX1 and OX2 orexin receptors ✅
- **(D)** Antagonist at postsynaptic H1 histamine receptors in brain
- **(E)** Agonist at postsynaptic D2 and D3 dopamine receptors in striatum

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: Suvorexant is a dual orexin receptor antagonist (DORA) that blocks OX1R and OX2R receptors, inhibiting wake-promoting orexin neuropeptides and facilitating sleep transition. Option (A) describes benzodiazepines/Z-drugs; Option (B) describes ramelteon; Option (D) describes pramipexole; Option (E) describes antihistamines.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Antagonist at dual orexin receptors (OX1R and OX2R)

---

## Question 99 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q44`

A 28-year-old male with narcolepsy complains of sudden loss of voluntary muscle tone triggered by laughter. What is the clinical term for this symptom?

- **(A)** Cataplexy ✅
- **(B)** Catalepsy
- **(C)** Chorea
- **(D)** Myoclonus
- **(E)** Athetosis

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Cataplexy is a sudden, brief loss of voluntary muscle tone triggered by strong emotions (such as laughter or excitement) with preserved consciousness, pathognomonic for Narcolepsy Type 1. Option (B) is fixed posture; Option (C) is involuntary dancing movement; Option (D) is sudden muscle twitching; Option (E) is slow writhing movement.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Cataplexy

---

## Question 100 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q45`

A 55-year-old male suffers an acute ischemic stroke in the left temporal lobe affecting the sublenticular portion of the internal capsule. Which central auditory fibers pass through the sublenticular internal capsule to reach the primary auditory cortex?

- **(A)** Projections from medial geniculate nucleus to transverse temporal gyrus ✅
- **(B)** Projections from lateral geniculate nucleus to calcarine fissure cortex
- **(C)** Projections from ventral posteromedial nucleus to postcentral gyrus
- **(D)** Projections from ventral posterolateral nucleus to precentral gyrus
- **(E)** Projections from anterior nucleus to cingulate gyral cortex

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The sublenticular part of the internal capsule carries auditory radiations connecting 5th order neurons in the medial geniculate nucleus (MGN) of the thalamus to the primary auditory cortex in Transverse Temporal (Heschl's) Gyrus. Option (B) travels in CN VIII; Option (C) forms trapezoid body/lateral lemniscus; Option (D) forms vestibulocerebellar tract; Option (E) ascends in brainstem.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Projections from the medial geniculate nucleus (MGN) to Transverse Temporal (Heschl's) Gyrus

## Question 101 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q49`

A 23-year-old female presents with normal distant vision and normal near vision, but reports that vertical and horizontal lines appear unevenly clear. Diagnosis of astigmatism is made. What optical remedy corrects astigmatism?

- **(A)** Spherical concave lens shifting focus posteriorly
- **(B)** Cylindrical lens correcting corneal astigmatism ✅
- **(C)** Spherical convex lens shifting focus anteriorly
- **(D)** Surgical phacoemulsification of the native lens
- **(E)** Topical parasympathomimetic eye drop therapy

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Astigmatism is caused by an irregular, asymmetrical curvature of the cornea or lens along specific meridians. It is corrected using a cylindrical lens that focuses light unevenly across different axes to compensate for corneal asymmetry. Concave lenses (A) correct myopia; Convex lenses (C) correct hyperopia/presbyopia; Lens removal (D) treats cataracts; Mydriatics (E) dilate pupil.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Cylindrical lens correcting corneal curvature irregularity

---

## Question 102 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q52`

A neuro-ophthalmologist tests pupillary responses in a comatose patient. Shining light into the right eye produces no response in either pupil. Shining light into the left eye produces prompt pupillary constriction in both eyes. What is the location of the lesion?

- **(A)** Right optic nerve carrying afferent sensory impulses from the right eye ✅
- **(B)** Right oculomotor nerve carrying efferent parasympathetic impulses to the right pupil
- **(C)** Left optic nerve carrying afferent sensory impulses from the left eye
- **(D)** Left oculomotor nerve carrying efferent parasympathetic impulses to the left pupil
- **(E)** Posterior commissure carrying decussating pretectal axons in the midbrain

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: When shining a penlight into the right eye produces no response in EITHER pupil (absent direct and consensual responses), the afferent sensory pathway from the right eye is non-functional. When shining light into the left eye produces prompt, normal pupillary constriction in BOTH pupils (intact direct and consensual responses), the left afferent pathway (left optic nerve) and BOTH efferent motor pathways (bilateral oculomotor nerves) are confirmed to be intact. Therefore, the lesion is located in the right optic nerve (CN II).

**High-Yield Pearl**: Absent direct and consensual responses upon illumination of one eye with intact bilateral responses upon illumination of the other localizes to the optic nerve of the unresponsive eye.

---

## Question 103 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q37`

A 68-year-old male suffers an ischemic stroke of the right posterior cerebral artery (PCA) affecting the right occipital pole. Examination reveals a left homonymous hemianopia with macular sparing. Why does macular vision remain intact in PCA territory visual cortex strokes?

- **(A)** Macular pathways project exclusively to the anterior temporal lobe cortex
- **(B)** Macular cortical area is expansive and receives collateral MCA blood supply ✅
- **(C)** Macular sensory fibers bypass the primary visual cortex to synapse in V2
- **(D)** Macular representation is localized entirely within the inferior parietal lobule
- **(E)** Dual sensory innervation from the ophthalmic nerve preserves central vision

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Macular sparing occurs in occipital PCA infarcts because the primary visual cortex area representing the macula (at the occipital pole) is anatomically large and has dual vascular supply from terminal branches of both PCA and MCA. MCA collateral flow maintains macular cortex perfusion during PCA occlusion. Options (A), (C), (D), and (E) are anatomically incorrect.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: The macular cortical area is large and receives collateral blood supply from the middle cerebral artery (MCA)

---

## Question 104 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q44`

A 64-year-old male presents with a midbrain stroke. Examination demonstrates that his left pupil is fixed and dilated, and he has ptosis of the left upper eyelid. When asked to look straight ahead, his left eye rests in a "down and out" position. Which cranial nerve nucleus or nerve root is damaged?

- **(A)** Left oculomotor nerve fascicles within the ventral midbrain tegmentum ✅
- **(B)** Left trochlear nerve fascicles within the dorsal midbrain tegmentum
- **(C)** Left abducens nerve fascicles within the caudal pontine tegmentum
- **(D)** Right oculomotor nerve fascicles within the rostral midbrain tegmentum
- **(E)** Left facial nerve fascicles looping around the abducens nucleus in the pons

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The combination of upper eyelid ptosis (paralysis of levator palpebrae superioris), a fixed dilated pupil (paralysis of parasympathetic sphincter pupillae), and an eye resting in a "down and out" position (unopposed action of the intact lateral rectus innervated by CN VI and superior oblique innervated by CN IV) is the classic presentation of a complete oculomotor nerve (CN III) palsy. A midbrain stroke involving the ventral tegmentum and exiting CN III fascicles (as seen in Weber syndrome when accompanied by contralateral hemiplegia) explains this presentation.

**High-Yield Pearl**: A complete CN III palsy causes severe ptosis, a fixed and dilated pupil, and a "down and out" resting eye position from unopposed CN IV and CN VI tone.

---

## Question 105 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q20`

A 45-year-old male chronic alcoholic presents with severe numbness and tingling in his feet, as well as weakness in both legs. Examination reveals loss of vibration and position sense in a stocking distribution, along with lower extremity spasticity, hyperreflexia, and bilateral upgoing toes. Laboratory evaluation confirms marked vitamin B12 deficiency (subacute combined degeneration). Which combination of spinal cord tracts is classically involved in this metabolic myelopathy?

- **(A)** Dorsal columns and lateral corticospinal tracts ✅
- **(B)** Anterior spinothalamic tracts and ventral horns
- **(C)** Lateral spinothalamic tracts and dorsal root ganglia
- **(D)** Anterior horn lower motor neurons and spinocerebellar tracts
- **(E)** Ventral white commissure and fasciculus cuneatus

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Vitamin B12 deficiency causes subacute combined degeneration (SCD) of the spinal cord, selectively involving the dorsal columns (loss of vibration and position sense, sensory ataxia) and lateral corticospinal tracts (UMN spasticity, hyperreflexia, bilateral Babinski signs). Option (B) causes anterior cord syndrome; Option (C) causes spinothalamic deficits; Option (D) causes motor neuron disease; Option (E) causes central syrinx deficits.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Dorsal columns and lateral corticospinal tracts

---

## Question 106 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q40`

A 33-year-old female presents with a lesion affecting the optic chiasm (e.g., pituitary macroadenoma). Which nerve fibers are interrupted at the optic chiasm?

- **(A)** Uncrossed temporal retinal fibers from both eyes
- **(B)** Decussating nasal retinal fibers from both eyes ✅
- **(C)** All fibers originating from the right eye exclusively
- **(D)** Auditory radiations from the thalamus
- **(E)** Sympathetic fibers from the superior cervical ganglion

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The optic chiasm is the decussation site where axons originating from the nasal hemiretinae of both eyes cross the midline. These crossing fibers carry information from the temporal (lateral) visual fields of both eyes, so compression at the chiasm produces bitemporal hemianopia. Uncrossed temporal fibers (A) pass laterally without crossing; Option (C) affects only one eye; Options (D) and (E) are non-visual pathways.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Decussating nasal retinal fibers from both eyes

---

## Question 107 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q23`

A 60-year-old male with a history of hypertension presents with mild vertigo, gait instability, dysmetria on left finger-to-nose testing, and skew deviation of the eyes. On gaze examination, he exhibits vertical and bi-directional horizontal nystagmus that does NOT fatigue and does NOT suppress with visual fixation. Audiometric evaluation reveals completely normal hearing bilaterally. Which localized central lesion best accounts for this patient's vertigo?

- **(A)** Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo of the posterior canal
- **(B)** Acute peripheral vestibular neuritis of the superior branch
- **(C)** Central ischemic stroke of the cerebellum or brainstem nuclei ✅
- **(D)** Ménière disease with fluctuating endolymphatic hydrops
- **(E)** Labyrinthitis with acute inflammatory cochlear involvement

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: Central vertigo features mild/variable vertigo, central neurological signs (ataxia, dysmetria, skew deviation), and non-suppressible, non-fatigable, vertical or bi-directional nystagmus without hearing loss. This presentation indicates a central cerebellar or brainstem stroke. Options (A), (B), and (D) represent peripheral vestibulopathies characterized by visual suppression and fatigability. Option (E) causes conductive hearing loss.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Ischemic stroke of the left cerebellum or brainstem vestibular nuclei

---

## Question 108 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q51`

A 33-year-old female presents with ptosis, miosis, and anhidrosis on the left side of her face. MRI shows an acute dissection of the left internal carotid artery. Postganglionic sympathetic fibers for facial sweating along the forehead and cheek travel along which arterial pathway?

- **(A)** External carotid artery plexus for the face and internal carotid plexus for the forehead ✅
- **(B)** Internal carotid artery plexus exclusively supplying the entire face and scalp
- **(C)** Vertebral artery plexus traversing the cervical transverse foramina to the brain
- **(D)** Subclavian arterial plexus within the posterior triangle of the cervical neck
- **(E)** Basilar arterial plexus supplying circumferential pontine and cerebellar branches

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Third-order sympathetic fibers emerge from the superior cervical ganglion and divide into two arterial pathways. Sudomotor (sweat) fibers supplying the majority of the face, cheek, and jaw travel with the external carotid artery (ECA) plexus along facial arterial branches. In contrast, sympathetic sudomotor fibers destined for the medial forehead, along with oculosympathetic fibers for the pupillary dilator and superior tarsal muscle, travel with the internal carotid artery (ICA) plexus through the carotid canal. Therefore, lesions of the internal carotid artery typically cause Horner syndrome with anhidrosis restricted strictly to the forehead or with completely spared facial sweating.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Facial sudomotor sympathetic fibers divide at the superior cervical ganglion: the ECA plexus supplies the lower face, while the ICA plexus supplies the forehead and orbit.

---

## Question 109 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q27`

A 35-year-old worker at a chemical manufacturing plant presents with acute vertigo and ataxia following an occupational exposure. Anatomical mapping of his central vestibular projections is performed. Second-order vestibular neurons sending uncrossed descending fibers via the lateral vestibulospinal tract (LVST) originate primarily from which specific brainstem nucleus?

- **(A)** Superior vestibular nucleus projecting to the ocular motor nuclei
- **(B)** Medial vestibular nucleus projecting to cervical spinal segments
- **(C)** Lateral vestibular nucleus projecting to ipsilateral spinal cord ✅
- **(D)** Inferior vestibular nucleus projecting to the cerebellar vermis
- **(E)** Abducens motor nucleus projecting to the ipsilateral lateral rectus

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: The lateral vestibulospinal tract (LVST) originates from second-order cell bodies in the lateral vestibular nucleus (Deiters nucleus) at the pontomedullary junction. LVST axons descend uncrossed through the spinal cord to excite motor neurons of antigravity extensor muscles. Option (A) projects via MLF to extraocular nuclei; Option (B) projects to cerebellum; Option (D) gives rise to MVST; Option (E) processes auditory signals.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Lateral vestibular nucleus (Deiters nucleus)

---

## Question 110 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q53`

A 40-year-old female presents with a 2-day history of severe, continuous vertigo, nausea, and staggering gait. She has no hearing loss or tinnitus. Neurological exam reveals horizontal nystagmus beating to the left, which suppresses with visual fixation. Brain MRI is normal. The patient is diagnosed with acute vestibular neuritis. What is the primary management strategy for this self-limiting condition?

- **(A)** Immediate surgical endolymphatic shunt decompression
- **(B)** Short-term symptomatic vestibular suppressants and antiemetics ✅
- **(C)** High-dose long-term daily aminoglycoside chemotherapy
- **(D)** Emergent suboccipital decompressive posterior fossa craniectomy
- **(E)** Long-term permanent dietary potassium restriction therapy

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Acute vestibular neuritis is a self-limiting post-viral inflammation of the vestibular division of CN VIII. Hearing is completely preserved. Management focuses on short-term symptomatic relief using vestibular suppressants (meclizine, benzodiazepines) and antiemetics for 3–5 days, followed by vestibular rehabilitation. Option (A) is inappropriate; Option (C) is ototoxic; Option (D) is for central stroke; Option (E) is for tumors.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Symptomatic relief with short-term vestibular suppressants (e.g., meclizine) and antiemetics

---

## Question 111 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q39`

A 45-year-old male is evaluated for weakness in his right hand. Examination demonstrates weakness of thumb abduction and finger spread. There is pronounced atrophy of the right thenar eminence. Reflexes in the right bicep and tricep are 1+. No Babinski sign is present. Sensory testing shows loss of pinprick and touch sensation along the medial hand and forearm (C8–T1 dermatomes). Which structure is damaged?

- **(A)** Left precentral gyrus lateral surface
- **(B)** Right ventral horn exclusively at C8–T1
- **(C)** Right spinal nerve / ventral and dorsal roots at C8–T1 ✅
- **(D)** Left corticospinal tract in the midbrain
- **(E)** Posterior limb of the internal capsule

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: LMN weakness and atrophy in the right hand combined with dermatomal sensory loss along the medial hand and forearm (C8–T1) indicate combined involvement of motor (ventral) and sensory (dorsal) root pathways at the C8–T1 segment (spinal nerve / nerve roots). Option (A) causes motor UMN deficits without dermatomal sensory loss; Option (B) causes pure motor LMN deficits; Option (D) causes contralateral UMN hemiplegia; Option (E) causes pure motor internal capsule hemiplegia.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right spinal nerve / ventral and dorsal roots at C8–T1

## Question 112 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q55`

An investigator studies EEG wave characteristics across sleep stages. Match the following wave patterns with their corresponding sleep/awake states: (1) Alpha waves, (2) Sleep spindles and K-complexes, (3) Delta waves, (4) Sawtooth waves.

- **(A)** 1: Awake relaxed eyes closed, 2: Stage 2 NREM, 3: Stage 3 NREM, 4: REM sleep ✅
- **(B)** 1: REM sleep, 2: Stage 3 NREM, 3: Stage 2 NREM, 4: Awake relaxed eyes closed
- **(C)** 1: Stage 2 NREM, 2: Stage 3 NREM, 3: REM sleep, 4: Awake relaxed eyes closed
- **(D)** 1: Stage 3 NREM, 2: REM sleep, 3: Awake relaxed eyes closed, 4: Stage 2 NREM
- **(E)** 1: Awake relaxed eyes closed, 2: REM sleep, 3: Stage 2 NREM, 4: Stage 3 NREM

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Match: (1) Alpha waves = Awake relaxed with eyes closed; (2) Sleep spindles and K-complexes = Stage 2 NREM; (3) Delta waves = Stage 3 NREM slow-wave sleep; (4) Sawtooth waves = REM sleep. Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) mispair wave patterns and sleep stages.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: 1: Awake relaxed eyes closed, 2: Stage 2 NREM, 3: Stage 3 NREM, 4: REM sleep

## Question 113 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q43`

A 50-year-old male presents with ptosis and miosis of the left eye following a neck dissection surgery. Anhidrosis is present on the left side of his face. Which ganglion houses the third-order cell bodies for postganglionic sympathetic fibers supplying the face and orbit?

- **(A)** Ciliary ganglion located in the posterior orbit adjacent to CN II
- **(B)** Superior cervical ganglion situated at the level of the C2 to C3 vertebrae ✅
- **(C)** Pterygopalatine ganglion located within the pterygopalatine fossa
- **(D)** Otic ganglion located in the infratemporal fossa medial to CN V3
- **(E)** Dorsal root ganglion located within the intervertebral foramen of C2

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: In the three-neuron oculosympathetic pathway, second-order preganglionic neurons ascend through the cervical sympathetic trunk to synapse onto third-order postganglionic cell bodies located in the superior cervical ganglion. The superior cervical ganglion is the largest sympathetic ganglion, situated in the upper neck at the level of the C2 and C3 vertebrae adjacent to the internal carotid artery. Axons of these third-order neurons then form postganglionic plexuses along the internal and external carotid arteries to reach the dilator pupillae, superior tarsal muscle, and facial sweat glands.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Postganglionic sympathetic cell bodies supplying the head, orbit, and pupil reside in the superior cervical ganglion at the C2–C3 vertebral level.

---

## Question 114 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q40`

An investigator records electrical activity in the pretectal nucleus during pupillary testing. Axons from each pretectal nucleus project to both the ipsilateral and contralateral Edinger-Westphal nuclei. Crossing fibers traveling to the contralateral Edinger-Westphal nucleus pass through which midbrain commissure?

- **(A)** Anterior commissure carrying interhemispheric temporal connections
- **(B)** Posterior commissure carrying decussating pretectal projection fibers ✅
- **(C)** Habenular commissure connecting bilateral epithalamic habenular nuclei
- **(D)** Corpus callosum splenium carrying interhemispheric occipital fibers
- **(E)** Optic chiasm decussating nasal retinal fibers from both eye retinas

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: In the pupillary light reflex arc, light stimulating retinal ganglion cells activates pretectal neurons in the rostral midbrain. To ensure that light in one eye produces equal pupillary constriction in both eyes (consensual reflex), axons from each pretectal nucleus project bilaterally: one uncrossed pathway directly to the ipsilateral Edinger-Westphal nucleus, and one crossed pathway through the posterior commissure to the contralateral Edinger-Westphal nucleus. Interruption of the posterior commissure impairs consensual responses while sparing direct ipsilateral constriction.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Consensual pupillary light reflex fibers decussate across the midbrain midline via the posterior commissure.

---

## Question 115 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q54`

An anatomical study traces retinal projections. Fibers originating from the temporal hemiretina of the left eye project to which anatomical structure?

- **(A)** Crosses at optic chiasm to right optic tract
- **(B)** Remains uncrossed and projects via left optic tract to left LGN ✅
- **(C)** Crosses at optic chiasm to left superior colliculus
- **(D)** Projects directly to right visual cortex V1
- **(E)** Synapses in Edinger-Westphal nucleus bilaterally

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Axons originating from the temporal hemiretina of the left eye carry signals from the nasal visual field of the left eye. These temporal retinal fibers remain UNCROSSED at the optic chiasm and ascend in the ipsilateral (left) optic tract to synapse in the left lateral geniculate nucleus (LGN). Options (A), (C), (D), and (E) violate uncrossed temporal fiber anatomy.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Remains uncrossed and projects via left optic tract to left LGN

---

## Question 116 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q23`

A 68-year-old male presents with severe lower back pain radiating down both legs, accompanied by urinary incontinence and bowel dysfunction that developed over the past 12 hours. Examination reveals sensory loss confined to the perianal and saddle region (S3–S5 dermatomes), loss of the anal wink reflex, and symmetrical lower extremity weakness with mixed upper and lower motor neuron signs. Which anatomical region of the spinal cord is acutely compressed in this patient?

- **(A)** Conus medullaris ✅
- **(B)** Cauda equina
- **(C)** Cervical enlargement
- **(D)** Mid-thoracic spinal cord
- **(E)** Precentral gyrus motor homunculus

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The conus medullaris is the terminal end of the spinal cord housing sacral segments (S1–S5) at L1–L2 vertebral level. Acute compression produces bilateral saddle anesthesia (S3–S5), early severe bowel/bladder dysfunction, loss of the anal wink reflex (S3–S4), and symmetrical lower extremity weakness with mixed UMN/LMN signs. Option (B) presents with asymmetric radicular pain and pure LMN signs; Option (C) causes quadriparesis; Option (D) causes thoracic sensory level and spastic paraplegia; Option (E) causes contralateral hemiparesis.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Conus medullaris

## Question 117 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q26`

A 19-year-old college student presents with excessive daytime sleepiness. On several occasions while standing in line at the cafeteria, he has experienced sudden episodes of falling asleep without warning. He also reports seeing terrifying monster-like figures in his room just as he is waking up in the morning. What term describes hallucinations that occur specifically upon awakening from sleep?

- **(A)** Hypnagogic hallucinations
- **(B)** Hypnopompic hallucinations ✅
- **(C)** Delirium tremens
- **(D)** Synesthesias
- **(E)** Illusionary palinopsia

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Hypnopompic hallucinations occur upon awakening from sleep (transition from sleep to wakefulness). Hypnagogic hallucinations occur while falling asleep (transition from wakefulness to sleep). Option (A) occurs when falling asleep; Option (C) is alcohol withdrawal; Option (D) is sensory cross-wiring; Option (E) is visual persistence.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Hypnopompic hallucinations
