## Question 217 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q38`

A 50-year-old female presents with episodic vertigo. During examination, the clinician performs the Dix-Hallpike test: the patient is lowered rapidly from a sitting position to a supine position with her head turned 45 degrees to the right and extended 30 degrees backward. After a 4-second latency, the patient experiences severe vertigo and horizontal-rotatory nystagmus beating toward the right ear, which fatigues and stops after 20 seconds. What is the clinical significance of the fatiguability and latency observed in this test?

- **(A)** Diagnostic of an acute central brainstem or cerebellar infarction
- **(B)** Characteristic of a benign peripheral vestibulopathy such as BPPV ✅
- **(C)** Pathognomonic for compressive vestibulocochlear nerve schwannoma
- **(D)** Indicative of toxic chemical labyrinthitis from ototoxic medication
- **(E)** Characteristic of progressive autoimmune inner ear disease

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: In Dix-Hallpike testing, a peripheral origin (BPPV) exhibits a 2- to 5-second latency period and fatiguability (nystagmus and vertigo cease within 30–60 seconds). Central lesions produce immediate, non-fatigable, non-habituating nystagmus. Option (A) describes central lesions; Option (C) is incorrect as BPPV does not cause nerve loss; Option (D) is central; Option (E) is incorrect.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Indicates a benign peripheral vestibulopathy (e.g., BPPV)

---

## Question 218 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q49`

A 35-year-old male presents with difficulty maintaining sleep. He is prescribed suvorexant. Suvorexant promotes sleep by antagonizing which receptors?

- **(A)** Orexin receptors ✅
- **(B)** Melatonin receptors
- **(C)** Dopamine receptors
- **(D)** Muscarinic receptors
- **(E)** Beta-adrenergic receptors

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Suvorexant is a dual orexin receptor antagonist (DORA) that promotes sleep by blocking wake-promoting orexin A and orexin B neuropeptides from binding to OX1R and OX2R receptors. Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) are not suvorexant targets.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Orexin receptors

---

## Question 219 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q51`

A 16-year-old high school student presents with extreme daytime drowsiness. Polysomnography reveals normal nocturnal sleep duration with intact sleep architecture. Multiple sleep latency test (MSLT) shows a mean sleep latency of 4 minutes with rapid entry into REM sleep. What is the diagnosis?

- **(A)** Narcolepsy type 1 with cataplexy ✅
- **(B)** Psychophysiological primary insomnia
- **(C)** Severe obstructive sleep apnea syndrome
- **(D)** Restless legs movement disorder
- **(E)** Slow-wave sleep night terror parasomnia

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Excessive daytime sleepiness with intact nocturnal sleep, mean sleep latency <8 minutes on MSLT, and rapid entry into REM sleep (SOREMPs) defines narcolepsy. Option (B) is nocturnal sleep initiation loss; Option (C) features apneas on polysomnogram; Option (D) features leg dysesthesias; Option (E) features N3 terrors.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Narcolepsy

---

## Question 220 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q48`

A 55-year-old female presents with a tumor compressing the left lateral geniculate nucleus (LGN) in the thalamus. What visual field deficit will be produced?

- **(A)** Right homonymous hemianopia ✅
- **(B)** Left homonymous hemianopia
- **(C)** Bitemporal hemianopia
- **(D)** Monocular left anopia
- **(E)** Right upper quadrantanopia

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The left lateral geniculate nucleus (LGN) processes all visual information from the right visual hemifield (ipsilateral temporal retina and contralateral decussating nasal retina). Complete destruction of the left LGN produces a complete right homonymous hemianopia. Left homonymous hemianopia (B) involves right LGN; Bitemporal (C) involves chiasm; Monocular loss (D) involves optic nerve; Right upper quadrantanopia (E) involves left Meyer loop.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right homonymous hemianopia

---

## Question 221 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q21`

A 25-year-old medical student tilts her head backward and forward while standing on a moving elevator that accelerates vertically upward. Which vestibular receptor organ is specialized to detect vertical linear acceleration and gravitational pull?

- **(A)** Ampulla of the horizontal semicircular canal
- **(B)** Macula of the vertically oriented saccule ✅
- **(C)** Macula of the horizontally oriented utricle
- **(D)** Crista ampullaris of the anterior canal
- **(E)** Basilar membrane of the apical cochlea

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Otolith organs contain maculae. The saccular macula is oriented vertically along the anatomical wall, specializing in detecting vertical linear acceleration (elevator movement) and gravity when upright. Utricular macula is horizontal, detecting horizontal linear acceleration. Option (A) detects rotation; Option (C) detects horizontal acceleration; Option (D) detects rotation; Option (E) processes sound frequency.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Macula of the saccule

---

## Question 222 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q32`

A 60-year-old male with obstructive sleep apnea presents with daytime somnolence and fatigue. Polysomnography demonstrates recurrent nocturnal airway collapse. What pathophysiological consequence results from recurrent apneas during sleep?

- **(A)** Transient hypoxemia and sleep fragmentation leading to daytime fatigue ✅
- **(B)** Hyperventilation causing persistent respiratory alkalosis
- **(C)** Direct destruction of alveolar septa by orexin deficiency
- **(D)** Autoimmune destruction of phrenic nerve lower motor neurons
- **(E)** Selective loss of growth hormone secretion during N3 sleep

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Obstructive sleep apnea involves recurrent pharyngeal collapse causing transient hypoxemia, hypercapnia, micro-arousals, and sleep fragmentation, which results in excessive daytime fatigue and elevated cardiovascular risk. Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) misrepresent OSA pathophysiology.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Transient hypoxemia and sleep fragmentation leading to daytime fatigue

---

## Question 223 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q21`

A 30-year-old factory worker presents with a loss of pain and temperature sensation across both shoulders and upper arms in a "cape-like" distribution. Tactile sensation, vibration, and joint position sense remain intact throughout the entire body. Strength in the upper extremities is currently 5/5, but muscle tone is normal. Magnetic resonance imaging reveals a fluid-filled cavity within the central canal of the cervical spinal cord (syringomyelia). Which neural pathway is directly compressed to produce this specific pattern of sensory loss?

- **(A)** Main sensory axons in the dorsal columns
- **(B)** Primary afferent axons crossing in the ventral white commissure ✅
- **(C)** Descending motor fibers in the lateral corticospinal tract
- **(D)** Lower motor neurons in the lateral ventral horn
- **(E)** Autonomic preganglionic fibers in the intermediolateral column

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Syringomyelia is a fluid-filled cavity (syrinx) expanding within the central canal of the spinal cord (most commonly cervical). It selectively compresses decussating second-order spinothalamic axons in the ventral white commissure, producing bilateral loss of pain and temperature sensation in a "cape-like" distribution across corresponding dermatomes (C5–T2). Dorsal columns remain intact, preserving fine touch and proprioception (dissociated sensory loss). Option (A) impairs fine touch/vibration; Option (C) causes lower limb spasticity; Option (D) causes LMN hand weakness; Option (E) causes Horner syndrome.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Primary afferent axons crossing in the ventral white commissure

---

## Question 224 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q42`

A 31-year-old female presents with left-sided facial weakness and left-sided hearing loss. Physical examination shows an inability to close her left eye or smile on the left side, loss of taste on the anterior two-thirds of her tongue on the left, and left sensorineural hearing loss. Where is the anatomical site of the lesion compressing these two cranial nerves together?

- **(A)** Jugular foramen at the posterior cranial base
- **(B)** Cerebellopontine angle at the internal acoustic meatus ✅
- **(C)** Foramen rotundum within the sphenoid greater wing
- **(D)** Superior orbital fissure at the sphenoid apex
- **(E)** Carotid canal within the petrous temporal bone

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: CN VII and CN VIII exit the pontomedullary junction together and traverse the cerebellopontine angle (CPA) into the internal auditory meatus. Lesions in this space (vestibular schwannoma) compress both nerves, causing sensorineural hearing loss/tinnitus/vertigo (CN VIII) and facial muscle weakness/taste loss (CN VII). Option (A) houses CN IX, X, XI; Option (C) transmits CN V3; Option (D) transmits CN III, IV, V1, VI; Option (E) transmits CN XII.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Cerebellopontine angle (CPA) / internal auditory meatus

---

## Question 225 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q32`

A 35-year-old woman is evaluated for a broad-based gait and frequent tripping. Neurological examination reveals increased muscle tone in both legs, with passive movement meeting maximal resistance at the onset of extension before suddenly giving way ("clasp-knife" spasticity). What electrophysiological change occurs in the spinal stretch reflex circuit following a chronic upper motor neuron lesion that causes this spasticity?

- **(A)** Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis causing combined upper and lower motor neuron loss
- **(B)** Myasthenia gravis causing autoantibody destruction of postsynaptic ACh receptors ✅
- **(C)** Guillain-Barré syndrome causing acute inflammatory peripheral demyelination
- **(D)** Lambert-Eaton syndrome causing presynaptic voltage-gated calcium channel loss
- **(E)** Polymyositis causing autoimmune inflammatory destruction of skeletal muscle

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Spasticity is characterized by velocity-dependent resistance to passive stretch. Descending UMN tracts (corticospinal and cortico-bulbo-spinal) deliver tonic inhibitory modulation to spinal motor circuits. Interruption of UMN pathways leaves spinal lower motor neurons uninhibited and hyper-reactive, causing unchecked monosynaptic reflex firing upon passive muscle stretch. Option (A) is incorrect as afferents remain intact; Option (C) causes LMN flaccidity; Option (D) describes Lambert-Eaton; Option (E) is incorrect as alpha motoneuron hyper-excitability drives spasticity.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Uninhibited hyper-reactivity of lower motor neurons due to loss of descending UMN inhibition

---

## Question 226 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q28`

A 29-year-old woman presents with severe daytime fatigue. A multiple sleep latency test (MSLT) is ordered. During the MSLT, the patient is given 5 scheduled nap opportunities spaced 2 hours apart. What parameter does the MSLT specifically quantify to evaluate excessive daytime sleepiness?

- **(A)** Maximum nadir of systemic arterial oxygen saturation on oximetry
- **(B)** Mean sleep latency to sleep onset and number of sleep-onset REM periods ✅
- **(C)** Amplitude and frequency of synchronized delta waves during N3 sleep
- **(D)** Periodic limb movement index per hour of total recorded sleep time
- **(E)** Total fractional percentage of cerebral blood flow during phasic REM

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The Multiple Sleep Latency Test (MSLT) measures daytime sleep latency (time to fall asleep) and presence of sleep-onset REM periods (SOREMPs) across 5 scheduled daytime naps to diagnose narcolepsy (<8 min latency, ≥2 SOREMPs). Options (A), (C), (D), and (E) are not the primary parameter quantified by MSLT.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Time elapsed from lights out to the onset of sleep (sleep latency) and occurrence of REM periods

---

## Question 227 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q8`

A neuroscientist evaluates sleep-promoting circuitry in a mammalian brain model. Lesions in a specific hypothalamic nucleus result in severe, chronic insomnia. This nucleus normally promotes sleep onset by releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) to inhibit the ascending reticular activating system (ARAS). Which nucleus is this?

- **(A)** Ventrolateral preoptic nucleus in the anterior hypothalamus ✅
- **(B)** Tuberomammillary nucleus in the posterior hypothalamus
- **(C)** Locus coeruleus in the rostral pontine tegmentum
- **(D)** Dorsal raphe nucleus in the rostral midbrain tegmentum
- **(E)** Pedunculopontine tegmental nucleus in the rostral pons

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The ventrolateral preoptic nucleus (VLPO) in the anterior hypothalamus is the primary sleep-promoting center. VLPO neurons release GABA and galanin to inhibit wakefulness centers in the ascending reticular activating system (ARAS). Lesions cause severe chronic insomnia. Option (B) promotes wakefulness via orexin; Option (C) secretes oxytocin/vasopressin; Option (D) is the circadian clock; Option (E) controls motor output.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Ventrolateral preoptic nucleus (VLPO)

---

## Question 228 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q22`

A 60-year-old male suffers a stroke affecting the left parietal lobe, damaging the superior optic radiations (dorsal loop). Which visual field deficit is expected?

- **(A)** Right upper homonymous quadrantanopia
- **(B)** Right lower homonymous quadrantanopia ✅
- **(C)** Bitemporal hemianopia
- **(D)** Monocular visual loss in left eye
- **(E)** Left lower homonymous quadrantanopia

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The superior optic radiations (dorsal loop) travel through the parietal lobe to the cuneus gyrus, carrying information from the upper retina (lower visual field). A lesion of the left parietal dorsal loop produces a right lower (inferior) homonymous quadrantanopia. Left temporal Meyer loop (A) causes right upper quadrantanopia; Chiasm (C) causes bitemporal loss; Left optic nerve (D) causes left monocular loss; Right parietal loop (E) causes left lower quadrantanopia.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right lower homonymous quadrantanopia

---

## Question 229 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q12`

A 43-year-old female presents with hoarseness, difficulty swallowing solids and liquids, and frequent coughing spells when drinking water. Examination of the pharynx demonstrates that at rest, the left palatal arch is drooping. When the patient is asked to say "Ah," the uvula deviates to the right side. The gag reflex is absent when the left posterior pharyngeal wall is stimulated but present when the right side is stimulated. Which of the following structures is damaged?

- **(A)** Right nucleus ambiguus
- **(B)** Left nucleus ambiguus ✅
- **(C)** Left hypoglossal nucleus
- **(D)** Right corticobulbar tract
- **(E)** Left trigeminal motor nucleus

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Lower motor neurons for CN IX and X reside in the nucleus ambiguus in the rostral medulla, innervating muscles of the soft palate, pharynx, and larynx. Damage to the left nucleus ambiguus causes LMN weakness of the left soft palate, causing it to droop. Upon phonation ("Ah"), the intact right levator veli palatini pulls the uvula away from the lesioned side (toward the right). Left CN X/IX motor loss also abolishes the efferent limb of the gag reflex on the left. Option (A) causes right-sided palatal weakness and uvular deviation to the left; Option (C) affects tongue movement; Option (D) causes minimal palatal deviation due to bilateral corticobulbar input; Option (E) affects muscles of mastication.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Left nucleus ambiguus

---

## Question 230 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q29`

A 70-year-old female presents with an acute stroke. Examination reveals that her eyes are locked in a position looking to the left. Which two anatomical lesions could independently produce a resting gaze preference toward the left side?

- **(A)** Left frontal eye field destructive stroke or right PPRF destructive stroke ✅
- **(B)** Right frontal eye field destructive stroke or left PPRF destructive stroke
- **(C)** Left oculomotor nucleus infarction or right trochlear nucleus infarction
- **(D)** Bilateral medial longitudinal fasciculi demyelinating plaques
- **(E)** Left optic nerve traumatic transection or right ciliary ganglion avulsion

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Conjugate horizontal eye deviation looking locked toward the left can arise from two distinct anatomical lesion locations: 1) A destructive cortical stroke involving the LEFT frontal eye field (FEF). The left FEF normally drives gaze to the right; its destruction allows the intact right FEF to drive the eyes to the left ("eyes look toward the hemispheric lesion"). 2) A destructive pontine stroke involving the RIGHT paramedian pontine reticular formation (PPRF). The right PPRF normally drives gaze to the right; its destruction allows the intact left PPRF to drive the eyes to the left ("eyes look away from the pontine lesion").

**High-Yield Pearl**: Conjugate horizontal gaze locked to the left occurs with a destructive left FEF stroke (toward the lesion) or a destructive right PPRF stroke (away from the lesion).

---

## Question 231 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q13`

An experimental study measures neuronal firing rates in the superior olivary complex of the caudal pons during acoustic stimulation. The investigators observe that neurons in the medial superior olive fire maximally when there is a minute microsecond delay between the arrival of a sound wave at the left ear versus the right ear. What primary auditory function is mediated by this binaural processing center?

- **(A)** Analysis of speech syntax and semantics
- **(B)** Discrimination of complex pitch harmonics
- **(C)** Sound localization in the horizontal plane ✅
- **(D)** Voluntary damping of the tympanic membrane
- **(E)** Transduction of linear vertical acceleration

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: The superior olivary complex in the caudal pons receives bilateral auditory input. The medial superior olive compares interaural time differences, and the lateral superior olive compares interaural intensity differences. This binaural processing enables sound localization in the horizontal plane. Option (A) is Wernicke area; Option (B) is primary cortex/cochlea; Option (D) is CN VII/V3 reflex; Option (E) is saccule macula.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Sound localization in the horizontal plane

---

## Question 232 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q45`

A 62-year-old male with severe insomnia is prescribed a short course of a non-benzodiazepine hypnotic agent. What is the mechanism of action of non-benzodiazepines like zolpidem and zaleplon?

- **(A)** Allosteric modulation of the GABA-A receptor at the benzodiazepine site ✅
- **(B)** Agonist activation of melatonin MT1 and MT2 G-protein coupled receptors
- **(C)** Competitive antagonist blockade at dual OX1 and OX2 orexin receptors
- **(D)** Antagonist blockade of postsynaptic H1 histamine receptors in cortex
- **(E)** Agonist activation of presynaptic D2 and D3 dopamine auto-receptors

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Non-benzodiazepines (zolpidem, zaleplon, eszopiclone) act as selective agonists at the alpha-1 subunit of GABAA receptors at the benzodiazepine site, increasing GABA-mediated inhibitory neurotransmission to induce sleep. Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) describe other drug classes.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Binding to the GABA-A receptor at the benzodiazepine site to facilitate GABA-A receptor opening

---

## Question 233 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q34`

A 48-year-old male presents with recurrent episodes of vertigo, tinnitus, and progressive hearing loss in his right ear. Over time, he notices that his hearing loss has become prominent for low-frequency tones. He is started on a low-sodium diet and a diuretic to reduce endolymphatic fluid pressure. What condition is being treated?

- **(A)** Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo
- **(B)** Ménière disease ✅
- **(C)** Vestibular schwannoma
- **(D)** Otitis media
- **(E)** Superior canal dehiscence

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Episodic vertigo, fluctuating low-frequency sensorineural hearing loss, tinnitus, and ear fullness define Ménière disease (endolymphatic hydrops). Management includes a low-sodium diet and diuretics to reduce endolymphatic fluid pressure. Option (A) is treated with Epley maneuver; Option (C) is managed surgically; Option (D) is treated with antibiotics; Option (E) requires surgical bone repair.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Ménière disease

---

## Question 234 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q47`

A 55-year-old male presents with a brainstem stroke. Examination reveals that when he attempts horizontal gaze to the right, neither eye moves to the right. When he attempts horizontal gaze to the left, his left eye abducts normally with nystagmus, but his right eye cannot adduct. What complex gaze syndrome is present ("one-and-a-half syndrome")?

- **(A)** Combined lesion of the right PPRF and the right medial longitudinal fasciculus ✅
- **(B)** Bilateral frontal eye field strokes involving the precentral motor cortex
- **(C)** Bilateral complete oculomotor nerve palsies from interpeduncular compression
- **(D)** Combined lesion of the optic chiasm and bilateral optic tract radiations
- **(E)** Isolated pinealoma compressing the dorsal tectum and superior colliculus

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The patient exhibits "one-and-a-half syndrome." On attempted horizontal gaze to the right, neither eye moves to the right (a complete rightward horizontal conjugate gaze palsy, representing "one"). On attempted horizontal gaze to the left, the left eye abducts normally with nystagmus, but the right eye fails to adduct (a right internuclear ophthalmoplegia, representing the "half"). This unique neuro-ophthalmological syndrome is caused by a single focal unilateral lesion in the caudal dorsal pontine tegmentum involving BOTH the right PPRF (or right abducens nucleus) AND the adjacent crossing right MLF.

**High-Yield Pearl**: One-and-a-half syndrome is caused by a unilateral pontine lesion damaging both the ipsilateral PPRF (conjugate gaze palsy) and ipsilateral MLF (internuclear ophthalmoplegia).

---

## Question 235 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q37`

A 65-year-old male presents with sudden hearing loss in his left ear. Audiometry confirms a severe left sensorineural hearing loss. Weber test lateralizes to the right (good) ear. Rinne test is positive bilaterally (AC > BC). An MRI of the brain demonstrates an ischemic stroke involving a small artery that branches off AICA to supply the inner ear structures, entering via the internal auditory meatus. Which artery is occluded?

- **(A)** Labyrinthine artery ✅
- **(B)** Middle meningeal artery
- **(C)** Ophthalmic artery
- **(D)** Anterior cerebral artery
- **(E)** Posterior spinal artery

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The labyrinthine artery (internal auditory artery, usually branching from AICA) supplies the membranous inner ear (cochlea and vestibular apparatus), entering via the internal auditory meatus with CN VII and VIII. Occlusion causes sudden monaural sensorineural hearing loss and acute vertigo. Option (B) supplies dura mater; Option (C) supplies orbit/retina; Option (D) supplies medial cortex; Option (E) supplies posterior cord.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Labyrinthine artery

---

## Question 236 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q25`

An 18-year-old male wrestler presents with weakness in his right upper extremity following a severe traction injury to his neck. Physical examination demonstrates marked weakness of forearm flexion and supination, along with an absent biceps reflex on the right side. Triceps and patellar reflexes are 2+ bilaterally. Muscle tone in the right arm is flaccid. Which of the following nerve roots is primary involved in the execution of the biceps stretch reflex arc?

- **(A)** C3–C4
- **(B)** C5–C6 ✅
- **(C)** C7–C8
- **(D)** L1–L2
- **(E)** L3–L4

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The biceps reflex evaluates C5–C6 spinal cord segments and the musculocutaneous nerve. Tapping the biceps tendon sends Ia afferents via the musculocutaneous nerve to C5–C6 dorsal horn, synapsing onto alpha motor neurons in C5–C6 ventral horn that transmit efferents to contract the biceps brachii. Option (A) innervates diaphragm (phrenic nerve); Option (C) evaluates C6–C8 (triceps); Option (D) evaluates L1–L2; Option (E) evaluates L3–L4.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: C5–C6

---

## Question 237 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q43`

A 45-year-old male suffers a temporal bone fracture. On physical examination, tapping the left mastoid process produces sound that is heard clearly, but when the tuning fork is held next to his left ear canal, he hears nothing (BC > AC; negative Rinne). The Weber test lateralizes to the left ear. Which structure was damaged by the temporal bone fracture?

- **(A)** Cochlear division of CN VIII
- **(B)** Middle ear ossicular chain or tympanic membrane ✅
- **(C)** Transverse temporal gyrus of Heschl
- **(D)** Medial geniculate nucleus
- **(E)** Superior vestibular nucleus

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Negative Rinne on the left (Bone Conduction > Air Conduction) and Weber lateralization to the left ear confirm left conductive hearing loss. Temporal bone fractures causing conductive loss disrupt middle ear mechanics (ossicular chain dislocation or eardrum perforation/hemotympanum). Option (A) causes sensorineural loss; Options (C) and (D) cause central processing deficits; Option (E) causes vestibular loss.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Middle ear ossicular chain or tympanic membrane

---

## Question 238 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q24`

A 54-year-old male undergoes surgical repair of a large abdominal aortic aneurysm. Postoperatively, he develops bilateral lower extremity paralysis and loss of pain and temperature sensation below the T10 level. Vibration, fine touch, and proprioception remain completely normal bilaterally in both legs. Which spinal cord syndrome has occurred as a complication of this surgery?

- **(A)** Posterior cord syndrome
- **(B)** Anterior cord syndrome ✅
- **(C)** Brown-Séquard syndrome
- **(D)** Syringomyelia
- **(E)** Subacute combined degeneration

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Ischemia of the anterior spinal artery (often following aortic aneurysm repair) causes Anterior Cord Syndrome, damaging the lateral corticospinal tracts (bilateral motor paralysis below lesion) and anterolateral system (bilateral loss of pain and temperature below lesion). The dorsal columns are spared as they are supplied by posterior spinal arteries, preserving fine touch and proprioception. Option (A) affects dorsal columns; Option (C) is a hemisection; Option (D) causes cape-like arm sensory loss; Option (E) affects dorsal and lateral columns without anterior cord pattern.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Anterior cord syndrome

---

## Question 239 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q27`

A neurologist evaluates a patient with suspected lower motor neuron pathology. The examiner gently strokes the skin around the anus, observing a visible contraction of the external anal sphincter muscle (anal wink reflex). Which spinal cord segment roots mediate both the afferent and efferent limbs of this spinal reflex?

- **(A)** L1–L2
- **(B)** L3–L4
- **(C)** L5–S1
- **(D)** S1–S2
- **(E)** S3–S4 ✅

**Correct Answer**: **(E)**

**Explanation**: The anal wink reflex evaluates lower sacral segments S3–S4 and the pudendal nerve. Stroking perianal skin stimulates sensory afferents carried by the pudendal nerve to S3–S4, synapsing onto motor neurons in the S3–S4 ventral horn that send efferents via the pudendal nerve to contract the external anal sphincter. Option (A) evaluates L1–L2; Option (B) evaluates L3–L4; Option (C) evaluates L5–S1; Option (D) evaluates S1–S2.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: S3–S4

## Question 240 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q32`

An 8-year-old girl is evaluated for a sore throat, fever, and left earache. Otoscopic examination demonstrates an intact, dull, amber-colored left tympanic membrane with fluid behind it. No purulent discharge or acute bulging is present. Weber test lateralizes to the left ear, and Rinne test shows bone conduction greater than air conduction on the left. What is the diagnosis?

- **(A)** Cholesteatoma with ossicular chain necrosis
- **(B)** Otitis media with serous middle ear effusion ✅
- **(C)** Otosclerosis with stapedial footplate fixation
- **(D)** Acoustic neuroma within the auditory canal
- **(E)** Presbycusis with cochlear hair cell atrophy

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Otitis media with effusion (OME) presents with non-purulent fluid behind an intact, dull, amber-colored tympanic membrane without acute bulging or fever. Fluid impedes ossicular vibration, producing conductive hearing loss (Weber to affected ear, negative Rinne). Option (A) presents with ear canal swelling and tragus pain; Option (C) causes vertigo and sensorineural loss; Option (D) is a keratinizing mass; Option (E) is age-related sensorineural loss.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Otitis media with effusion (serous otitis media)

---

## Question 241 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q31`

A neurophysiologist maps the primary visual cortex (V1). The cuneus gyrus is located on the medial surface of the occipital lobe superior to the calcarine sulcus. Which portion of the optic radiations projects to the cuneus gyrus?

- **(A)** Meyer loop in the temporal lobe carrying upper visual field inputs
- **(B)** Dorsal loop in the parietal lobe carrying lower visual field inputs ✅
- **(C)** Nasal retinal decussating fibers carrying temporal visual field inputs
- **(D)** Temporal retinal uncrossed fibers carrying nasal visual field inputs
- **(E)** Auditory radiations from the medial geniculate nucleus to cortex

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The cuneus gyrus is the upper bank of the calcarine sulcus, receiving superior optic radiations (dorsal loop) that travel through the parietal lobe carrying signals from the upper retina (lower visual field). Meyer loop (A) projects to the lingual gyrus; Nasal fibers (C) cross at chiasm; Uncrossed fibers (D) carry ipsilateral temporal retina; Auditory radiations (E) project to Heschl's gyrus.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Superior optic radiations / dorsal loop (parietal lobe) carrying lower visual field

---

## Question 242 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q1`

A 58-year-old male presents with progressive hearing loss in his right ear over the past two years. Physical examination reveals normal pinnae and uninflamed, clear tympanic membranes bilaterally. A 512 Hz tuning fork is placed on the midline of his forehead (Weber test), and the sound lateralizes distinctly to the left ear. When the tuning fork is placed on the right mastoid process and then moved next to the right external auditory canal (Rinne test), air conduction is determined to be greater than bone conduction (positive test bilaterally). Which of the following anatomical structures is the primary site of pathology in this patient?

- **(A)** Malleus, incus, and stapes ossicular chain
- **(B)** External auditory canal epidermoid lining
- **(C)** Organ of Corti hair cells or cochlear division of CN VIII ✅
- **(D)** Tympanic membrane pars tensa
- **(E)** Epithelial lining of the Eustachian tube

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: Rinne test is positive bilaterally (Air Conduction > Bone Conduction), ruling out conductive hearing loss. Weber test lateralizes to the left ear (the unaffected ear). In sensorineural hearing loss, Weber lateralizes away from the impaired ear to the good ear. Sensorineural loss results from pathology in the organ of Corti hair cells or cochlear division of CN VIII. Option (A) causes conductive loss (negative Rinne); Option (B) causes conductive loss; Option (D) causes conductive loss; Option (E) causes middle ear effusion and conductive loss.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Organ of Corti hair cells or cochlear division of CN VIII

---

## Question 243 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q40`

A 50-year-old female presents with a 3-month history of progressive stiffness in her left leg. When she walks, her left leg remains extended and she swings it outward in a semicircular arc to prevent her toes from dragging on the ground (circumductive gait). Examination shows 4/5 strength in the left leg, hyperactive patellar reflex (3+), and an upgoing left plantar reflex. Upper extremity and facial motor exams are normal. Where is the lesion located?

- **(A)** Right medial precentral gyrus / paracentral lobule ✅
- **(B)** Left lateral precentral gyrus
- **(C)** Left ventral horn of the lumbar spinal cord
- **(D)** Right facial motor nucleus in the caudal pons
- **(E)** Right lateral primary motor cortex

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The lower extremity motor representation resides on the medial surface of the primary motor cortex in the paracentral lobule. Because corticospinal pathways decussate in the medullary pyramids, an UMN lesion in the right medial precentral gyrus / paracentral lobule causes spastic hemiparesis and circumductive gait isolated to the contralateral (left) leg. Option (B) controls right arm/face; Option (C) causes LMN flaccid paralysis in left leg; Option (D) causes facial paralysis; Option (E) represents hand/arm/face.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right medial precentral gyrus / paracentral lobule

---

## Question 244 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q34`

A 62-year-old female presents with a complete lesion of the left facial nerve (CN VII) at the stylomastoid foramen. How does this LMN facial nerve lesion impact the pupillary light reflex?

- **(A)** Corneal reflex is lost on the left side, while pupillary light reflex remains normal ✅
- **(B)** Pupillary light reflex is lost on the left side, while corneal reflex remains normal
- **(C)** Both corneal and pupillary light reflexes are lost on the left side completely
- **(D)** Both pupils become fixed and dilated with complete bilateral cycloplegia
- **(E)** Consensual pupillary light reflex is selectively lost across the midline

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The facial nerve (CN VII) supplies somatic branchial motor innervation to the muscles of facial expression, including the orbicularis oculi, which constitutes the efferent motor limb of the corneal blink reflex (afferent limb is CN V1). A complete peripheral lesion of the left facial nerve at the stylomastoid foramen abolishes the efferent blink response in the left eye, so neither eye can blink on the left when either cornea is touched. In contrast, the pupillary light reflex is mediated by the optic nerve (CN II, afferent) and oculomotor nerve (CN III, efferent); it does NOT involve the facial nerve at all and remains completely intact.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Facial nerve (CN VII) lesions impair the efferent limb of the corneal blink reflex (orbicularis oculi) but leave the pupillary light reflex (CN II / CN III) completely normal.

---

## Question 245 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q33`

A 67-year-old male presents with difficulty localizing where sounds are coming from while walking in traffic. He can hear that a car horn is blowing, but cannot determine whether the car is approaching from his left or his right. Auditory processing testing demonstrates an inability to process interaural time and intensity differences. Which central brainstem auditory nucleus is primarily responsible for sound localization?

- **(A)** Spiral ganglion
- **(B)** Superior olivary complex ✅
- **(C)** Medial geniculate nucleus
- **(D)** Transverse temporal gyrus
- **(E)** Inferior vestibular nucleus

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Sound localization requires processing interaural time differences (medial superior olive) and interaural intensity differences (lateral superior olive). The superior olivary complex in the caudal pons is the first central structure to receive binaural auditory input. Option (A) is monaural; Option (C) is a thalamic relay; Option (D) is auditory cortex; Option (E) is vestibular.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Superior olivary complex

---

## Question 246 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q54`

An auditory neurophysiologist traces the pathway of acoustic processing from the brainstem to the cerebral cortex. Arrange the following central auditory structures in the correct order of ascending signal transmission: (1) Inferior colliculus, (2) Cochlear nucleus, (3) Medial geniculate nucleus, (4) Superior olivary complex, (5) Transverse temporal gyrus.

- **(A)** 2 -> 4 -> 1 -> 3 -> 5 ✅
- **(B)** 1 -> 2 -> 3 -> 4 -> 5
- **(C)** 4 -> 2 -> 1 -> 3 -> 5
- **(D)** 2 -> 1 -> 4 -> 3 -> 5
- **(E)** 3 -> 2 -> 1 -> 4 -> 5

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The ascending central auditory pathway sequence is: Spiral ganglion -> Cochlear nucleus (2) -> Superior olivary complex (4) via trapezoid body -> Inferior colliculus (1) via lateral lemniscus -> Medial geniculate nucleus (3) -> Transverse temporal gyrus (5) via sublenticular internal capsule. Sequence: 2 -> 4 -> 1 -> 3 -> 5.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: 2 -> 4 -> 1 -> 3 -> 5

---

## Question 247 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q14`

A 19-year-old college student presents with myopia (nearsightedness). Refractive testing reveals that light rays entering his eye are focused in front of the retina. What structural abnormality of the eye causes myopia?

- **(A)** Eye axial length too short
- **(B)** Eye axial length too long ✅
- **(C)** Irregular curvature of the cornea
- **(D)** Stiffening of the crystalline lens
- **(E)** Absence of the iris

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Myopia (nearsightedness) occurs when the axial length of the eyeball is structurally too long relative to its refractive power, causing parallel light rays from distant objects to focus in front of the retina. Eye too short (A) causes hyperopia. Corneal irregularity (C) causes astigmatism. Lens stiffening (D) causes presbyopia. Aniridia (E) is iris absence.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Eye axial length too long

---

## Question 248 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q41`

A 33-year-old male involved in a high-speed collision sustains a severe hyperextension injury to his neck. Physical examination demonstrates marked weakness in both upper extremities (strength 2/5), whereas strength in both lower extremities is relatively preserved (4/5). Loss of pain and temperature sensation is present across the neck and arms, while proprioception is preserved throughout. What spinal cord syndrome is represented?

- **(A)** Anterior cord syndrome
- **(B)** Central cord syndrome ✅
- **(C)** Posterior cord syndrome
- **(D)** Brown-Séquard syndrome
- **(E)** Cauda equina syndrome

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Central cord syndrome (common in cervical hyperextension injuries) selectively damages the central region of the cervical spinal cord, affecting medial fibers of lateral corticospinal tracts (arm representation) and decussating spinothalamic fibers in the ventral white commissure. This results in motor weakness disproportionately greater in the upper extremities than lower extremities, with cape-like pain and temperature loss and spared dorsal columns. Option (A) causes total motor/pain loss below level; Option (C) causes proprioceptive loss; Option (D) causes hemisection deficits; Option (E) affects lumbosacral roots.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Central cord syndrome

---

## Question 249 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q42`

A 61-year-old male suffers a stroke involving the superior division of the left middle cerebral artery (MCA), injuring the left parietal lobe white matter. Which pathway is damaged, and what visual field deficit results?

- **(A)** Meyer loop damage causing right upper quadrantanopia
- **(B)** Dorsal loop damage causing right lower quadrantanopia ✅
- **(C)** Optic chiasm damage causing complete bitemporal hemianopia
- **(D)** Optic tract damage causing left homonymous hemianopia
- **(E)** Lingual gyrus damage causing left upper quadrantanopia

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: An MCA stroke affecting the left parietal lobe white matter damages the superior optic radiations (dorsal loop), which carry lower visual field fibers from the upper retina. This produces a right lower (inferior) homonymous quadrantanopia ("pie on the floor"). Meyer loop (A) travels through temporal lobe; Chiasm (C) causes bitemporal loss; Optic tract (D) causes homonymous hemianopia; Lingual gyrus (E) is PCA territory.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Superior optic radiations (dorsal loop); Right lower quadrantanopia

---

## Question 250 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q30`

A 72-year-old male presents with difficulty reading fine print. He states that he has good distance vision, but near vision has gradually worsened over the past 10 years. Refractive testing confirms presbyopia. What is the physiological cause of presbyopia?

- **(A)** Excessive progressive elongation of the axial length of the globe
- **(B)** Age-related loss of lens elasticity and stiffening of the capsule ✅
- **(C)** Progressive corneal endothelial decompensation and stromal clouding
- **(D)** Elevation of intraocular pressure causing optic nerve cupping
- **(E)** Selective apoptotic degeneration of peripheral rod photoreceptors

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Presbyopia is an age-related physiological accommodation deficit caused by progressive stiffness and loss of elasticity of the crystalline lens, preventing the lens from rounding up to focus on near objects. Eyeball elongation (A) causes myopia; Corneal clouding (C) causes vision loss; IOP elevation (D) causes glaucoma; Ganglion cell loss (E) occurs in optic neuropathy.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Age-related loss of lens elasticity and stiffening of the crystalline lens

---

## Question 251 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q2`

A 48-year-old woman is evaluated for progressive muscle weakness and localized twitching in her arms over the past six months. Examination reveals pronounced muscle wasting in the intrinsic muscles of both hands, along with visible, spontaneous muscle twitches under the skin. Deep tendon reflexes in the biceps and brachioradialis are absent bilaterally. Electromyography confirms the presence of fibrillations and fasciculations in the upper extremity muscles. Which of the following pathophysiological mechanisms directly accounts for the loss of muscle mass in this patient?

- **(A)** Interruption of descending inhibitory reticulospinal tracts
- **(B)** Denervation-induced degradation of muscle fibers from lower motor neuron loss ✅
- **(C)** Continuous hyperactivation of the monosynaptic stretch reflex arc
- **(D)** Ischemic necrosis of Betz cells in the precentral gyrus
- **(E)** Autoimmune destruction of post-synaptic nicotinic acetylcholine receptors

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Muscle wasting, fasciculations (spontaneous involuntary twitches of single motor units), fibrillations, and absent deep tendon reflexes define a lower motor neuron (LMN) lesion. Alpha motor neurons in the spinal ventral horns provide neurotrophic support and tonic activation to skeletal muscle. Loss of these motoneurons deprives muscle tissue of neurotrophic support, leading to denervation atrophy. Option (A) causes spasticity; Option (C) reflects uninhibited UMN stretch reflexes; Option (D) produces UMN signs without fasciculations or LMN muscle wasting; Option (E) describes myasthenia gravis, which presents with fatigable weakness without fasciculations or areflexia.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Denervation-induced degradation of muscle fibers from lower motor neuron loss

---

## Question 252 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q3`

A 28-year-old woman with a 3-year history of multiple sclerosis presents with diplopia when looking to the left. Physical examination reveals normal conjugate eye movements when looking to the right. However, on attempted leftward horizontal gaze, the left eye abducts normally with fine horizontal nystagmus, but the right eye fails to adduct past the midline. When tested for convergence during accommodation, both eyes adduct fully to the midline without difficulty. Where is the anatomical lesion located?

- **(A)** Right abducens nucleus in the caudal pontine tegmentum
- **(B)** Right medial longitudinal fasciculus in the brainstem tegmentum ✅
- **(C)** Left oculomotor nucleus in the rostral midbrain tegmentum
- **(D)** Left paramedian pontine reticular formation in the caudal pons
- **(E)** Right frontal eye field within the precentral motor cortex

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Internuclear ophthalmoplegia (INO) is caused by a lesion of the medial longitudinal fasciculus (MLF), the heavily myelinated brainstem tract that coordinates conjugate horizontal gaze by transmitting signals from the abducens nucleus (PPRF) to the contralateral oculomotor nucleus (medial rectus subnucleus). The affected MLF is named for the side that fails to adduct. Here, on leftward horizontal gaze, the right eye fails to adduct past the midline, while the left eye abducts normally with horizontal nystagmus; convergence during accommodation is intact because convergence pathways bypass the MLF and project directly from cortical visual association areas to the oculomotor nuclei. Multiple sclerosis is the leading cause of bilateral or unilateral INO in young adults.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Internuclear ophthalmoplegia (INO) localizes to the MLF on the side of the adduction deficit; convergence is preserved because accommodation pathways bypass the MLF.

---

## Question 253 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q43`

A 29-year-old male undergoes a complete transection of the right optic tract. What visual field defect will be observed on formal perimetry?

- **(A)** Right homonymous hemianopia
- **(B)** Left homonymous hemianopia ✅
- **(C)** Bitemporal hemianopia
- **(D)** Right upper homonymous quadrantanopia
- **(E)** Monocular blindness in the right eye

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: The right optic tract carries visual information from the ipsilateral temporal retina (right eye) and contralateral nasal retina (left eye), representing the complete left visual hemifield. Complete transection of the right optic tract produces a complete left homonymous hemianopia. Right homonymous hemianopia (A) involves left optic tract; Bitemporal hemianopia (C) involves chiasm; Right upper quadrantanopia (D) involves left Meyer loop; Monocular loss (E) involves optic nerve.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Left homonymous hemianopia

---

## Question 254 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q50`

A 70-year-old male presents with double vision. Examination shows that his right eye is deviated medially at rest. When asked to look to the right, the right eye cannot abduct. Facial sensation and facial motor function are normal. Where is the lesion located?

- **(A)** Right abducens nerve causing isolated failure of right lateral rectus abduction ✅
- **(B)** Right facial nerve causing ipsilateral flaccid paralysis of facial muscles
- **(C)** Left paramedian pontine reticular formation causing bilateral gaze palsy
- **(D)** Right oculomotor nerve causing ptosis and a fixed and dilated pupil
- **(E)** Left frontal eye field causing conjugate eye deviation toward the left

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: An inward (medial) deviation of the right eye at rest (esotropia) with an isolated inability to abduct the right eye past the midline, in the absence of any other cranial nerve or long tract findings, localizes to the right abducens nerve (CN VI). The abducens nerve innervates solely the lateral rectus muscle. When the lateral rectus is paralyzed, unopposed resting tone of the medial rectus adducts the eye toward the nose, resulting in horizontal binocular diplopia.

**High-Yield Pearl**: Medial strabismus (esotropia) at rest with isolated abduction failure localizes to the abducens nerve (CN VI).

---

## Question 255 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q41`

A 34-year-old female presents with difficulty sleeping due to painful, persistent involuntary teeth grinding during the night. Her husband reports a loud grinding sound. Dental exam shows severe wear of the molar enamel. During which sleep stage does bruxism primarily occur?

- **(A)** Awake state
- **(B)** N1 stage NREM
- **(C)** N2 stage NREM ✅
- **(D)** N3 stage NREM
- **(E)** REM sleep

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: Nocturnal teeth grinding (bruxism) occurs predominantly during N2 stage NREM sleep. Options (A), (B), (D), and (E) are not the primary sleep stage for bruxism.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: N2 stage NREM

---

## Question 256 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q10`

A 58-year-old female presents with a destructive lesion involving the right Paramedian Pontine Reticular Formation (PPRF / horizontal gaze center) in the caudal pons. Which pattern of gaze paralysis will be observed on physical examination?

- **(A)** Inability to execute voluntary conjugate horizontal gaze to the right ✅
- **(B)** Inability to execute voluntary conjugate horizontal gaze to the left
- **(C)** Selective paralysis of vertical upward conjugate eye movements
- **(D)** Isolated failure of left eye adduction with right abducting nystagmus
- **(E)** Bilateral complete pupillary dilation unresponsive to illumination

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The paramedian pontine reticular formation (PPRF) serves as the brainstem horizontal gaze center. Located in the caudal pontine tegmentum adjacent to the abducens nucleus, each PPRF coordinates ipsilateral conjugate horizontal gaze: right PPRF activation fires the right abducens nucleus, which stimulates the right lateral rectus and sends crossing MLF fibers to activate the left medial rectus. A destructive lesion of the right PPRF abolishes voluntary horizontal saccades toward the right side. At rest, unopposed contralateral PPRF drive causes the eyes to deviate AWAY from the pontine lesion (toward the left side).

**High-Yield Pearl**: Destructive lesions of the paramedian pontine reticular formation (PPRF) cause paralysis of conjugate horizontal gaze toward the side of the lesion.

---

## Question 257 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q3`

A 45-year-old female presents for evaluation of daytime fatigue. A full-night polysomnogram is performed. The continuous EEG tracing reveals periods characterized by prominent sleep spindles (12–14 Hz bursts) and sudden, high-voltage K-complexes. Teeth grinding (bruxism) is most likely to occur during this specific stage. Which sleep stage is depicted?

- **(A)** Awake state
- **(B)** N1 stage NREM
- **(C)** N2 stage NREM ✅
- **(D)** N3 stage NREM
- **(E)** REM sleep

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: N2 (Stage 2 NREM) accounts for ~45–55% of total nocturnal sleep. EEG displays two pathognomonic waveforms: sleep spindles (12–14 Hz bursts) and K-complexes (high-voltage biphasic waves). Involuntary nocturnal teeth grinding (bruxism) occurs predominantly during N2 sleep. Option (A) displays beta/alpha waves; Option (B) displays theta waves; Option (D) displays delta waves; Option (E) displays desynchronized sawtooth waves.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: N2 stage NREM

---

## Question 258 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q50`

A 75-year-old male presents with an acute stroke involving the left posterior cerebral artery (PCA) affecting the left cuneus gyrus (above the calcarine sulcus). What visual field defect will be detected?

- **(A)** Right lower homonymous quadrantanopia ✅
- **(B)** Right upper homonymous quadrantanopia
- **(C)** Bitemporal hemianopia
- **(D)** Left lower homonymous quadrantanopia
- **(E)** Complete left monocular anopia

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: The cuneus gyrus is the upper bank of the calcarine sulcus in the primary visual cortex, receiving parietal optic radiations carrying signals from the upper retina (lower visual field). A stroke of the left cuneus gyrus produces a right lower (inferior) homonymous quadrantanopia. Right upper quadrantanopia (B) involves lingual gyrus; Bitemporal (C) involves chiasm; Left lower quadrantanopia (D) involves right cuneus; Monocular loss (E) involves optic nerve.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right lower homonymous quadrantanopia

## Question 259 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q47`

A 60-year-old female presents with progressive hearing loss and occasional dizziness. A tuning fork exam shows a positive Rinne test bilaterally (AC > BC). Weber test lateralizes to the left ear. Audiometry confirms a right-sided sensorineural hearing loss. An MRI of the brain is ordered to evaluate for a vestibular schwannoma. What is the characteristic appearance of a vestibular schwannoma on contrast-enhanced MRI?

- **(A)** Non-enhancing fluid-filled cavity in the central spinal cord
- **(B)** Well-circumscribed, brightly enhancing mass at the cerebellopontine angle ✅
- **(C)** Ring-enhancing lesion in the basal ganglia with surrounding edema
- **(D)** Multiple demyelinating white matter plaques periventricularly
- **(E)** Pituitary stalk compression with a calcified suprasellar mass

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Contrast-enhanced MRI demonstrates vestibular schwannomas as well-circumscribed, intensely enhancing masses centered in the cerebellopontine angle (CPA), often extending into and widening the internal auditory meatus. Option (A) is syringomyelia; Option (C) is brain abscess/glioma; Option (D) is multiple sclerosis; Option (E) is craniopharyngioma.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Well-circumscribed, brightly enhancing mass at the cerebellopontine angle

---

## Question 260 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q14`

A 60-year-old male with long-standing hypertension presents with acute dysarthria and left-sided body weakness. On oral examination, when the patient is asked to extend his tongue, it protrudes significantly to the right side. Inspection reveals no tongue atrophy or fasciculations. Facial exam reveals a right-sided lower face droop with normal forehead wrinkling bilaterally. Which of the following is the anatomical site of the lesion?

- **(A)** Left hypoglossal nerve peripheral fibers in the deep submandibular tissue
- **(B)** Left corticobulbar and corticospinal tracts in the posterior limb of internal capsule ✅
- **(C)** Right hypoglossal motor nucleus situated in the dorsal rostral medulla
- **(D)** Right corticobulbar projection tract traversing the ventral caudal pons
- **(E)** Bilateral facial motor nuclei situated in the ventrolateral pontine tegmentum

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Corticobulbar projections to the hypoglossal nucleus (CN XII) are predominantly crossed (contralateral). An UMN lesion interrupting the left corticobulbar tract disconnects the right hypoglossal nucleus. Upon extension, the healthy left genioglossus pushes the tongue forward and shifts it contralateral to the UMN lesion (toward the right/weak side). Concomitant right lower facial droop (forehead spared) and right body weakness confirm a left-sided hemispheric/internal capsule stroke affecting UMN corticospinal and corticobulbar fibers. Option (A) causes ipsilateral tongue atrophy and fasciculations; Option (C) causes right tongue LMN atrophy; Option (D) causes left face/tongue weakness; Option (E) causes bilateral total face paralysis.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Left corticobulbar and corticospinal tracts in the internal capsule/brainstem

---

## Question 261 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual Reflexes` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q39`

A 35-year-old male presents with a pupil abnormality. In bright light, his left pupil remains dilated, whereas his right pupil constricts normally. When the room lights are brightened further, the anisocoria increases (left pupil fails to constrict in light). Which autonomic pathway is impaired, and in which eye?

- **(A)** Parasympathetic pupillomotor pathway impairment involving the left eye ✅
- **(B)** Sympathetic pupillodilator pathway impairment involving the right eye
- **(C)** Sympathetic pupillodilator pathway impairment involving the left eye
- **(D)** Primary retinal photoreceptor degeneration involving the right eye
- **(E)** Intermediolateral cell column destruction involving the T1 spinal segment

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: In bright illumination, normal pupils constrict briskly to minimize retinal light entry via parasympathetic activation of the sphincter pupillae muscles. If the left pupil remains dilated in bright light while the right pupil constricts normally, and the anisocoria worsens as light intensity increases, the defective pupil is the left one, and the underlying impairment is in the parasympathetic pupillomotor system (e.g., CN III lesion, uncal herniation, or pharmacological mydriasis).

**High-Yield Pearl**: Failure of pupillary constriction in bright light localizes the pathology to the efferent parasympathetic fibers of the oculomotor nerve (CN III).

---

## Question 262 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q10`

A 19-year-old female with narcolepsy undergoes a Multiple Sleep Latency Test (MSLT) following an overnight polysomnogram. Which set of diagnostic findings on the MSLT confirms the diagnosis of narcolepsy?

- **(A)** Mean sleep latency >15 minutes with complete absence of REM sleep
- **(B)** Mean sleep latency <8 minutes with multiple sleep-onset REM periods ✅
- **(C)** Persistent low-amplitude delta wave bursts during waking EEG
- **(D)** Severe prolongation of slow-wave sleep exceeding 40 percent
- **(E)** Complete absence of sleep spindles and K-complexes in N2 sleep

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Multiple Sleep Latency Test (MSLT) diagnosis of narcolepsy requires mean sleep latency <8 minutes across 5 nap opportunities combined with two or more sleep-onset REM periods (SOREMPs), reflecting pathological daytime sleepiness and rapid REM intrusion. Option (A) represents normal alertness; Option (C) is non-diagnostic; Option (D) excludes narcolepsy; Option (E) reflects fibrositis.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Mean sleep latency <8 minutes and two or more sleep-onset REM periods (SOREMPs)

---

## Question 263 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q2`

A 30-year-old male undergoes polysomnography. The EEG recording shows low-voltage, mixed-frequency brain waves, and the patient easily awakens when his name is called, reporting that he was only "dosing off." Which stage of non-rapid eye movement (NREM) sleep is represented, and which characteristic EEG wave pattern is observed?

- **(A)** N1 stage; theta waves ✅
- **(B)** N2 stage; sleep spindles and K-complexes
- **(C)** N3 stage; delta waves
- **(D)** REM sleep; sawtooth waves
- **(E)** Awake state; beta waves

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: N1 (Stage 1 NREM sleep) is the lightest sleep stage marking transition from wakefulness. EEG shows low-voltage, mixed-frequency theta waves (4–7 Hz). Patients easily awaken with light stimuli, often reporting they were only "dosing off." Option (B) defines N2 sleep; Option (C) defines N3 sleep; Option (D) defines REM sleep; Option (E) defines alert wakefulness.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: N1 stage; theta waves

## Question 264 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q48`

A 52-year-old female presents with sudden onset of right-sided body weakness and left eye movement abnormality. Examination reveals that her left eye is fixed in a "down and out" position with a dilated left pupil that does not respond to light. Motor exam shows 2/5 spastic weakness in the right arm and right leg, with hyperreflexia and an upgoing right plantar response. Which region of the brainstem contains the single lesion responsible for this presentation?

- **(A)** Left ventral rostral midbrain involving exiting CN III fibers and crus cerebri ✅
- **(B)** Right caudal pons involving CN VI nucleus and lateral spinothalamic tract
- **(C)** Left rostral medulla involving hypoglossal nucleus and medial lemniscus
- **(D)** Right mid-pons involving trigeminal motor nucleus
- **(E)** Left caudal midbrain involving trochlear nucleus

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: A single lesion in the left ventral rostral midbrain damages exiting oculomotor (CN III) fascicles (causing ipsilateral LMN CN III palsy: "down and out" eye, ptosis, fixed dilated pupil) and the adjacent crus cerebri (causing contralateral UMN spastic hemiplegia of the arm/leg and lower face). Option (B) causes CN VI/VII palsies in the pons; Option (C) causes medial medullary syndrome (CN XII and pyramid); Option (D) causes CN V motor palsy; Option (E) causes CN IV palsy in caudal midbrain.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Left ventral rostral midbrain involving exiting CN III fibers and crus cerebri

---

## Question 265 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q16`

A 24-year-old medical student is practicing neurological examination techniques on a classmate. When testing deep tendon reflexes, tapping the patellar tendon with a reflex hammer stretches the quadriceps muscle, eliciting a sudden extension of the leg at the knee. Which spinal cord segment levels are directly evaluated by this specific reflex arc?

- **(A)** C5–C6
- **(B)** C6–C8
- **(C)** L1–L2
- **(D)** L3–L4 ✅
- **(E)** S1–S2

**Correct Answer**: **(D)**

**Explanation**: The patellar reflex (knee-jerk) evaluates L3–L4 spinal segments and the femoral nerve. Tapping the patellar tendon stretches the quadriceps, sending Group Ia afferents via L3–L4 dorsal roots to synapse directly onto alpha motor neurons in the L3–L4 ventral horn, contracting the quadriceps. Option (A) evaluates C5–C6 (biceps); Option (B) evaluates C6–C8 (triceps); Option (C) evaluates L1–L2 (cremasteric); Option (E) evaluates S1–S2 (Achilles).

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: L3–L4

---

## Question 266 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q17`

A 50-year-old male undergoing a routine physical examination is found to have an absent Achilles tendon reflex on the right side. Tapping the right Achilles tendon fails to produce plantarflexion of the foot. The left Achilles reflex is 2+. Strength in ankle plantarflexion is slightly diminished on the right. Which spinal root level is primarily responsible for the execution of the Achilles reflex?

- **(A)** L1–L2
- **(B)** L3–L4
- **(C)** L5
- **(D)** S1–S2 ✅
- **(E)** S3–S4

**Correct Answer**: **(D)**

**Explanation**: The Achilles tendon reflex (ankle-jerk) tests S1–S2 spinal segments and the tibial nerve. Stretching the Achilles tendon activates muscle spindle afferents entering at S1–S2, synapsing onto lower motor neurons that transmit efferents via the tibial nerve (S1–S2) to contract gastrocnemius/soleus muscles and plantarflex the foot. Option (A) evaluates L1–L2; Option (B) evaluates L3–L4; Option (C) evaluates L5; Option (E) evaluates S3–S4 (anal wink).

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: S1–S2

---

## Question 267 of 275
**Topic**: `Visual System` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q52`

A 38-year-old male presents with visual complaints. Examination demonstrates a loss of the temporal visual field in the left eye and loss of the temporal visual field in the right eye (bitemporal hemianopia). A compression of decussating nasal fibers at which location causes this deficit?

- **(A)** Optic chiasm ✅
- **(B)** Left optic tract
- **(C)** Right optic tract
- **(D)** Left Meyer loop
- **(E)** Right dorsal loop

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Bitemporal hemianopia ("tunnel vision") is caused by compression or damage to the optic chiasm. The optic chiasm contains crossing axons originating from the nasal hemiretinae of both eyes, which carry light signals from the temporal visual fields bilaterally. Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) produce homonymous or quadrantic field loss.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Optic chiasm

---

## Question 268 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q45`

A 40-year-old male presents with a hoarse voice and difficulty coughing. Examination shows that his soft palate droops on the right side, and when he says "Ah," his uvula pulls toward the left. Examination of the tongue shows no atrophy or deviation. Shoulder shrug is normal bilaterally. Which cranial nerve nucleus is damaged?

- **(A)** Left hypoglossal nucleus
- **(B)** Right nucleus ambiguus ✅
- **(C)** Left nucleus ambiguus
- **(D)** Right trigeminal motor nucleus
- **(E)** Right facial motor nucleus

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Lower motor neurons in the nucleus ambiguus (CN IX, X) innervate the levator veli palatini. A right nucleus ambiguus lesion causes right soft palate drooping. On phonation ("Ah"), the intact left levator veli palatini contracts unopposed, pulling the uvula toward the healthy (left) side, away from the lesion. Option (A) causes tongue deviation to the left; Option (C) causes uvula deviation to the right; Option (D) causes jaw deviation; Option (E) causes facial paralysis.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right nucleus ambiguus

---

## Question 269 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q53`

A 58-year-old female presents with difficulty closing her right eye and a droop on the right side of her mouth. Physical exam demonstrates that she cannot wrinkle her right forehead, raise her right eyebrow, close her right eye, or smile on the right side. She also reports that sound seems uncomfortably loud in her right ear. Body strength and sensation are normal bilaterally. What is the location of the lesion?

- **(A)** Left primary motor cortex precentral gyrus facial homunculus
- **(B)** Right facial nerve peripheral fibers within the facial canal ✅
- **(C)** Left corticobulbar tract fibers within the posterior internal capsule
- **(D)** Right trigeminal sensory nerve fibers within the mandibular division
- **(E)** Left facial motor nucleus in the caudal pontine tegmentum

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: A peripheral LMN lesion of the right facial nerve (Bell palsy) causes total right-sided facial paralysis affecting both upper (forehead, eye closure) and lower (smile, mouth) facial muscles. Hyperacusis occurs due to involvement of the nerve to the stapedius in the facial canal. Option (A) causes right lower facial droop with forehead sparing; Option (C) causes contralateral lower face droop and body hemiplegia; Option (D) causes contralateral lower face droop; Option (E) causes palatal paralysis.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right facial nerve (CN VII) in the facial canal / stylomastoid foramen

---

## Question 270 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q17`

A 42-year-old woman presents with episodic, severe vertigo lasting 2 to 3 hours per episode, accompanied by fluctuating low-frequency hearing loss, roaring tinnitus, and a feeling of "fullness" in her left ear. Over the past year, she has experienced 6 such attacks, which leave her exhausted. Audiometry during an acute flare confirms left-sided low-frequency sensorineural hearing loss. Which of the following pathophysiological mechanisms is responsible for Ménière disease?

- **(A)** Displacement of calcium carbonate otoconia into the posterior semicircular canal
- **(B)** Endolymphatic hydrops characterized by excess endolymph within the labyrinth ✅
- **(C)** Viral inflammatory demyelination involving the superior division of CN VIII
- **(D)** Microvascular thrombosis of the labyrinthine artery within the internal meatus
- **(E)** Autoimmune destruction of inner hair cells within the basilar membrane

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: Ménière disease is characterized by episodic vertigo (lasting hours), fluctuating low-frequency sensorineural hearing loss, tinnitus, and ear fullness. The underlying pathophysiology is endolymphatic hydrops (impaired resorption and excess pressure of endolymph in the membranous labyrinth). Option (A) causes conductive loss; Option (C) causes BPPV; Option (D) causes mastoiditis; Option (E) causes AICA stroke.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Hydrops / excess accumulation of endolymph in the endolymphatic space

---

## Question 271 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q5`

A healthy 28-year-old volunteer is monitored during a sleep study. After approximately 90 minutes of sleep, the patient enters a state characterized by rapid eye movements under closed eyelids, complete voluntary muscle atonia, vivid dreaming, increased cerebral oxygen consumption, and sawtooth waves on EEG. Which of the following physiological changes also occurs specifically during this sleep stage (REM)?

- **(A)** Marked increase in skeletal muscle tone
- **(B)** Penile or clitoral tumescence ✅
- **(C)** Maximum secretion of growth hormone
- **(D)** Predominance of high-voltage delta waves
- **(E)** Peak frequency of sleep walking episodes

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: REM sleep features high central nervous system activity with somatic muscle atonia. EEG shows low-voltage desynchronized sawtooth waves. Physiological hallmarks include rapid eye movements, muscle atonia, vivid dreaming, increased cerebral oxygen consumption, and nocturnal penile/clitoral tumescence. Option (A) is incorrect as muscle tone is absent (atonia); Option (C) occurs during N3 sleep; Option (D) defines N3 sleep; Option (E) occurs during N3 sleep.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Penile or clitoral tumescence

---

## Question 272 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q16`

A 56-year-old patient with restless legs syndrome (RLS) is evaluated in the clinic. Serum iron levels are replenished, but symptoms persist. Which class of medications is recommended as first-line pharmacotherapy for moderate-to-severe primary restless legs syndrome?

- **(A)** Non-benzodiazepine hypnotics (e.g., zolpidem)
- **(B)** Dopamine agonists (e.g., pramipexole) or alpha-2-delta ligands (gabapentin) ✅
- **(C)** Orexin receptor antagonists (e.g., suvorexant)
- **(D)** Tricyclic antidepressants (e.g., amitriptyline)
- **(E)** First-generation H1 antihistamines (e.g., diphenhydramine)

**Correct Answer**: **(B)**

**Explanation**: First-line pharmacotherapy for moderate-to-severe primary RLS (after addressing iron deficiency) includes dopamine agonists (pramipexole, ropinirole) or alpha-2-delta calcium channel ligands (gabapentin, pregabalin). Option (A) treats insomnia; Option (C) treats insomnia; Option (D) worsens RLS; Option (E) antihistamines significantly worsen RLS.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Dopamine agonists (e.g., pramipexole) or alpha-2-delta ligands (gabapentin)

---

## Question 273 of 275
**Topic**: `Sleep & Sleep Disorders` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q31`

An 8-year-old boy is brought to his pediatrician because he frequently wets the bed (nocturnal enuresis) 2 to 3 times per week. Physical examination, urinalysis, and ultrasound are completely normal. Nocturnal enuresis occurs primarily during which stage of sleep?

- **(A)** Awake state
- **(B)** N1 stage NREM
- **(C)** N2 stage NREM
- **(D)** N3 stage NREM ✅
- **(E)** REM sleep

**Correct Answer**: **(D)**

**Explanation**: Nocturnal enuresis (bedwetting) is an NREM parasomnia that occurs predominantly during N3 slow-wave deep sleep. Options (A), (B), (C), and (E) do not represent the primary sleep stage for nocturnal enuresis.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: N3 stage NREM

---

## Question 274 of 275
**Topic**: `UMN / LMN` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q13`

A 38-year-old male is evaluated following a stab wound to the posterior neck. Physical examination demonstrates weakness when shrugging the right shoulder against resistance and weakness when turning the head to the left against resistance. The rest of the neurological exam is unremarkable. Where is the lesion located?

- **(A)** Right spinal accessory nucleus and peripheral nerve root fibers ✅
- **(B)** Right hypoglossal motor nucleus within the dorsal rostral medulla
- **(C)** Left corticobulbar tract traversing the posterior limb of internal capsule
- **(D)** Left primary motor cortex homunculus representation within precentral gyrus
- **(E)** Right ambiguus nucleus supplying branchial branch motor innervation

**Correct Answer**: **(A)**

**Explanation**: Lower motor neurons of CN XI reside in the accessory nucleus of the upper cervical spinal cord (C1–C5) and innervate the sternocleidomastoid (SCM) and trapezius muscles. The SCM turns the head to the opposite side (right SCM turns head to the left), while the trapezius elevates the ipsilateral shoulder. Damage to the right CN XI or its nucleus produces LMN weakness of the right trapezius (impaired right shoulder shrug) and right SCM (impaired head rotation to the left). Option (B) affects corticobulbar pathways; Option (C) affects tongue; Option (D) affects soft palate; Option (E) is a sensory dorsal root ganglion.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Right spinal accessory motor nucleus/nerve (CN XI)

---

## Question 275 of 275
**Topic**: `Auditory & Vestibular Systems` | **Original Unit Q#**: `Q3`

A 24-year-old woman is evaluated following an acute blast injury near an explosion. Otoscopic examination demonstrates perforation of the right tympanic membrane and minor disruption of the ossicular chain in the middle ear cavity. Which of the following best describes the physiological consequence of losing the normal mechanical impedance-matching function provided by the middle ear ossicles (malleus, incus, stapes)?

- **(A)** Total abolition of nerve action potential generation in the spiral ganglion
- **(B)** Retrograde degeneration of the primary auditory cortex in Heschl's gyrus
- **(C)** Significant loss of acoustic wave amplification prior to reaching the fluid-filled cochlea ✅
- **(D)** Selective loss of low-frequency sound transduction with complete preservation of high frequencies
- **(E)** Inability of stereocilia to hyperpolarize in response to mechanical deflection

**Correct Answer**: **(C)**

**Explanation**: Sound waves in air must be transmitted into perilymph within the inner ear. The middle ear ossicles and tympanic membrane function as an impedance-matching system, amplifying sound pressure ~20- to 30-fold (~30 dB). Disruption of ossicular continuity or eardrum perforation causes significant conductive hearing loss due to loss of acoustic amplification. Option (A) is incorrect because action potentials can still be generated if sound is amplified or conducted via bone; Option (B) does not occur; Option (D) causes generalized conductive loss; Option (E) is incorrect as hair cell mechanotransduction remains functional.

**High-Yield Pearl**: High-Yield Point: Significant loss of acoustic wave amplification prior to reaching the fluid-filled cochlea

---
